Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change · LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) · Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement
Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) Actions on Objectives High
Mimikatz, comsvcs.dll MiniDump, or any non-Windows process opening LSASS.
ATT&CKT1003.001 T1003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceEvents
DeviceEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType == "OpenProcessApiCall"
| where FileName =~ "lsass.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","csrss.exe",
                                          "svchost.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe",
                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent EventID 4663 = object access. AccessMask 0x10 / 0x40 / 0x1010
// indicate read access to lsass.exe — credential-dump signature. Audit
// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4663
| where ObjectName endswith "\lsass.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ObjectName, ProcessName,
          AccessMask, AccessReason
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: LSASS Process Access Outside Microsoft-Signed Tooling
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d04
status: experimental
description: |
    Anything opening lsass.exe with read-memory access that isn't a
    Microsoft signed component (Defender, csrss, etc.) is suspect.
    Mimikatz / Pypykatz / nanodump signature.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1003/001/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1003.001
    - attack.credential-access
logsource:
    category: process_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection:
        TargetImage|endswith: '\lsass.exe'
    filter_known_good:
        SourceImage|endswith:
            - '\MsSense.exe'
            - '\MsMpEng.exe'
            - '\csrss.exe'
            - '\svchost.exe'
            - '\wininit.exe'
            - '\services.exe'
            - '\lsm.exe'
            - '\SearchProtocolHost.exe'
    condition: selection and not filter_known_good
falsepositives:
    - Third-party EDR / forensic tools — extend the filter list
level: high
ENDPOINT

Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement Actions on Objectives High
psexec / paexec / smbexec / wmic /node — service install over SMB from remote host.
ATT&CKT1021.002 T1569.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
   or (FileName =~ "wmic.exe" and ProcessCommandLine has "/node:")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by Timestamp desc

SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688            // process-create (audit-policy required)
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\psexec.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\psexesvc.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\paexec.exe"
   or (NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe" and CommandLine has "/node:")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: PsExec / SMB Lateral Movement Tooling Execution
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d03
status: experimental
description: |
    PsExec, paexec, smbexec, csexec, remcom — admin-grade tooling that
    pivots over SMB. Pair with `wmic /node:` for the full cluster.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1021/002/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1569/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1021.002
    - attack.t1569.002
    - attack.lateral-movement
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_psexec:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\psexec.exe'
            - '\psexec64.exe'
            - '\psexesvc.exe'
            - '\paexec.exe'
            - '\csexec.exe'
            - '\remcom.exe'
            - '\smbexec.py'
    selection_wmic_remote:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\wmic.exe'
        CommandLine|contains:
            - '/node:'
    condition: selection_psexec or selection_wmic_remote
falsepositives:
    - Sysadmins running approved remote-management tooling — wrap with
      a known-admin-host watchlist
level: high

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Shai-Hulud / GhostAction malicious workflow artifact dropped on runner or repo checkout
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Network egress from CI/build host to GhostAction secret-exfil infrastructure · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] CI runner process POSTing secrets to GhostAction exfil host via curl/wget/node · OAuth consent / suspicious app grant
Network egress from CI/build host to GhostAction secret-exfil infrastructure AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts outbound connections from self-hosted GitHub Actions runners (or any host) to the GhostAction campaign's exfiltration infrastructure — the 45.139.104.115 / 216.126.225.129 IPs and the bold-dhawan/objective-hopper .plesk.page and carte-avantage.com domains that received 3,325+ stolen Actions secrets via curl POST. Pure IOC match on confirmed campaign infra, so any hit is a near-certain secret-dump in flight. Rationale: Uses the article's pre-extracted GhostAction IPs (45.139.104.115, 216.126.225.129) and domains (bold-dhawan/objective-hopper.plesk.page, carte-avantage.com), all independently confirmed by GitGuardian/BleepingComputer as the campaign's secret-exfil endpoints — hard IOCs, near-zero benign contact. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.gitguardian.com/ghostaction-campaign-3-325-secrets-stolen/ • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/hackers-steal-3-325-secrets-in-ghostaction-github-supply-chain-attack/ • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/ghostaction-campaign-over-3-000-secrets-stolen-through-malicious-github-workflows
ATT&CKT1567 T1041
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researcher or malware sandbox intentionally connecting to the IOC hosts for validation
  • Threat-intel enrichment/beaconing-check tooling that resolves or pings known-bad domains
  • Firewall logging a blocked/denied connection attempt (still worth reviewing, not a true compromise)
  • A future benign tenant reusing the 45-139-104-115.plesk.page shared-hosting range after takedown
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","SenseIR.exe","MsMpEng.exe")
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the source host — confirm whether it is a self-hosted GitHub Actions runner or CI build agent; pull the initiating process and command line. 2) Contain: block the IOC IPs/domains at egress, isolate the runner, and immediately rotate every secret that runner had access to (npm/PyPI/DockerHub/cloud tokens). 3) Hunt: enumerate all repos whose workflows executed on that runner, review recent .github/workflows commits for injected exfil steps, and pivot to UC2/UC3 on the same host.
let iocIPs = dynamic(["45.139.104.115","216.126.225.129"]);
let iocDomains = dynamic(["bold-dhawan.45-139-104-115.plesk.page","objective-hopper.45-139-104-115.plesk.page","carte-avantage.com"]);
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteIP in (iocIPs) or RemoteUrl has_any (iocDomains)
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteIP, RemoteUrl, RemotePort
| order by Timestamp desc

| order by TimeGenerated desc

Shai-Hulud / GhostAction malicious workflow artifact dropped on runner or repo checkout AIDelivery Medium
Hunts the file-write of the Shai-Hulud worm's signature workflow (shai-hulud-workflow.yml) and its bundle.js payload inside a repo/.github/workflows or runner working directory, plus the article's SHA1 acac5a9854650c4ae2883c4740bf87d34120c038. These are the planted supply-chain artifacts that harvest GitHub Actions secrets; the filename + hash are highly specific. Rationale: Keys on the Shai-Hulud worm's confirmed artifact name shai-hulud-workflow.yml (ReversingLabs/Datadog) plus the article's own SHA1 acac5a9854650c4ae2883c4740bf87d34120c038, scoping bundle.js to CI/runner paths to keep it high-fidelity for the supply-chain drop the article warns about. Cross-checked against: • https://www.reversinglabs.com/blog/shai-hulud-worm-npm • https://securitylabs.datadoghq.com/articles/shai-hulud-2.0-npm-worm/ • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/npm-supply-chain-attack/
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1204.003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A researcher cloning a compromised repo into a lab machine for analysis
  • EDR/AV quarantine or backup software creating a copy of the malicious file (write event on a security tool path)
  • A remediation/scanner tool writing the IOC filename as a signature sample
  • A developer intentionally checking in a same-named benign workflow (rare given the exact name)
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsMpEng.exe","MsSense.exe","veeam.backup.shell.exe")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the writing process (git, node, runner agent) and the repo/path; check whether the workflow already executed on this host. 2) Contain: isolate the runner, remove/quarantine the file, and rotate all secrets the affected repo/runner could read (npm publishing tokens first — Shai-Hulud reuses them to publish trojanized packages). 3) Hunt: search the org's repos for the same filename/hash and for recent commits by unfamiliar contributors adding .github/workflows files across many repos in a short window.

| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName =~ "shai-hulud-workflow.yml"
   or SHA1 == "acac5a9854650c4ae2883c4740bf87d34120c038"

| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA1, SHA256, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, TargetFileSHA1
| order by TimeGenerated desc

CI runner process POSTing secrets to GhostAction exfil host via curl/wget/node AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts a process on a self-hosted runner or build host invoking curl/wget/node/powershell with the GhostAction exfil hosts embedded in the command line — the on-runner footprint of the injected workflow step that curl-POSTs the harvested secret context to attacker infrastructure. Combines execution telemetry with confirmed campaign IOCs in the invocation string. Rationale: Matches the GhostAction exfil hosts appearing directly in a process command line — the runner-side signature of the curl POST that GitGuardian documented shipping secrets to these domains; command-line IOC presence is high-fidelity regardless of the interpreter used. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.gitguardian.com/ghostaction-campaign-3-325-secrets-stolen/ • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/hackers-steal-3-325-secrets-in-ghostaction-github-supply-chain-attack/
ATT&CKT1059 T1567 T1552.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • SOC analyst manually running curl against the IOC host to validate takedown/sinkhole status
  • Threat-intel enrichment or connectivity-check script that references the IOC in its arguments
  • Detection-engineering test/purple-team replay of the GhostAction payload in a lab
  • Backup or copy operation that includes the IOC string in a filename argument
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","SenseIR.exe") and AccountName !endswith "$"
Response runbook
1) Triage: capture the full command line and parent process; determine if this ran inside a workflow step on a runner vs an interactive analyst session. 2) Contain: kill/isolate the host, block the IOC hosts at egress, and rotate every secret exposed to that workflow run (prioritize npm/PyPI publishing tokens to prevent downstream package poisoning). 3) Hunt: correlate with UC1 network hits on the same host, review the triggering workflow's git history for the injected curl step, and check package registries for unexpected publishes using the potentially stolen tokens.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("acac5a9854650c4ae2883c4740bf87d34120c038") or SHA1 in~ ("acac5a9854650c4ae2883c4740bf87d34120c038") or MD5 in~ ("acac5a9854650c4ae2883c4740bf87d34120c038")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("acac5a9854650c4ae2883c4740bf87d34120c038") or SHA1 in~ ("acac5a9854650c4ae2883c4740bf87d34120c038") or MD5 in~ ("acac5a9854650c4ae2883c4740bf87d34120c038")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["acac5a9854650c4ae2883c4740bf87d34120c038"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

NodeBB remote federated-user profile lookup (ActivityPub XSS #1 trigger) AIDelivery Medium
Hunts inbound requests to NodeBB's /user/<name>@<domain> route, which forces the server to webfinger-resolve and fetch a remote ActivityPub actor whose icon.url is rendered unescaped into an og:image <meta> tag (buildMetaTag sink). A victim clicking such a link to an attacker-controlled instance is the delivery vector for the reflected XSS fixed in NodeBB 4.14.0. Surfaces which external handles/domains are being resolved so an analyst can spot lures to attacker.tld / ephemeral tunnel instances. Rationale: Anchored on the exact NodeBB federation route /user/<name>@<domain> from the article's PoC (https://nodebb.local/user/xss@attacker.tld) — the sole inbound signal that forces the server to fetch and render a remote actor's unescaped icon.url. Handles both raw '@' and URL-encoded '%40'. Cross-checked against: • https://community.nodebb.org/topic/19387/nodebb-v4.14.0-federation-regression-fixes-security-fixes-and-more • https://github.com/NodeBB/NodeBB/security/advisories/GHSA-wx69-rvg3-x7fc
ATT&CKT1189 T1059.007
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate federation: real users browsing genuine remote Fediverse/NodeBB/Lemmy actors (e.g. someuser@mastodon.social)
  • Search-engine or link-preview crawlers hitting shared federated profile URLs
  • Fediverse relay/aggregator bots resolving remote handles
  • Admins testing federation against known-good partner instances
  • Users pasting handles copied from other platforms
Suppression filter
| where RemoteHandleDomain !in~ ("mastodon.social","lemmy.world","lemmy.ml")   // extend with your org's known-good federation partners
Response runbook
1) Triage: extract RemoteHandleDomain from the hits; check whether the domain is a known federation partner or a newly-seen/ephemeral host (e.g. *.trycloudflare.com, freshly-registered). Correlate the client IP/user who requested it. 2) Contain: if the target NodeBB is <4.14.0, expedite upgrade (fix commit 4c4bf76 escapes federated data); temporarily disable ActivityPub or block outbound resolution to the suspicious domain at the egress proxy. 3) Hunt: pivot to UC 'Outbound ActivityPub actor fetch' for the same domain, and review admin session activity for any account whose browser rendered the profile (session hijack via alert(origin) equivalent).
AppServiceHTTPLogs
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where CsUriStem startswith "/user/"
| where CsUriStem contains "@" or CsUriStem contains "%40"
| extend RemoteHandleDomain = extract(@"/user/[^/@]+(?:@|%40)([A-Za-z0-9._-]+)", 1, CsUriStem)
| where isnotempty(RemoteHandleDomain)
| summarize Requests = count(), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated), Clients = make_set(CIp, 20) by RemoteHandleDomain, CsUriStem, CsHost
| order by FirstSeen desc
title: NodeBB Remote Federated User Profile Lookup (ActivityPub XSS trigger)
id: 6f2e9d21-8c47-4b3a-9c1e-4d7a2f0b9e11
description: Inbound request to NodeBB /user/<name>@<domain> route that resolves a remote ActivityPub actor, the delivery path for the federation icon.url XSS fixed in NodeBB 4.14.0
references:
  - https://www.aikido.dev/blog/eight-high-severity-vulnerabilities-nodebb
  - https://community.nodebb.org/topic/19387/nodebb-v4.14.0-federation-regression-fixes-security-fixes-and-more
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/07/22
tags:
  - attack.t1189
  - attack.t1059.007
logsource:
  category: webserver
detection:
  selection:
    cs-uri-stem|startswith: '/user/'
  handle_at:
    cs-uri-stem|contains:
      - '@'
      - '%40'
  condition: selection and handle_at
level: medium
CLOUD

NodeBB server outbound ActivityPub/webfinger fetch to ephemeral tunnel or new domain AIExploitation Medium
Detects the NodeBB server making outbound /.well-known/webfinger and /ap/actor ActivityPub discovery requests to attacker-controlled federation instances — the server-side fetch that pulls the malicious actor JSON (icon.url XSS payload) or a rogue signing key. Flags ephemeral Cloudflare tunnel destinations (observed in the research setup) and federation partners never seen before in the baseline window. Rationale: Uses the article's concrete ActivityPub discovery paths (/.well-known/webfinger, /ap/actor/) plus the pre-extracted ephemeral-tunnel IOC (*.trycloudflare.com, the host attacker.tld was fronted by) and a newly-seen-partner anti-join. Server-side outbound fetch is the pivot that distinguishes exploitation from normal browsing. Cross-checked against: • https://community.nodebb.org/topic/19387/nodebb-v4.14.0-federation-regression-fixes-security-fixes-and-more
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1102
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate first-time federation with a new but benign remote instance
  • Developers/researchers self-hosting a test Fediverse instance on a Cloudflare tunnel
  • Relay/Lemmy federation resuming after the 4.14.0 upgrade contacting many new hosts at once
  • Link-unfurl or preview services proxied through the same egress path
  • Security scanners probing the org's ActivityPub endpoints
Suppression filter
| where DstHostname !in~ ("mastodon.social","lemmy.world","lemmy.ml")   // known-good federation partners
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the source is the NodeBB host and the destination is not a sanctioned federation partner; resolve the domain age/hosting (ephemeral tunnels + <30-day domains are suspicious). 2) Contain: block egress to the destination host and, if the icon.url XSS is unpatched (<4.14.0), disable ActivityPub until upgraded. 3) Hunt: correlate with UC1 inbound /user/<h>@<domain> requests for the same domain, and check admin/moderator sessions rendered around the fetch time for token/session anomalies indicating a successful stored/reflected XSS.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","nodejs.exe","node")
| where RemoteUrl has "/.well-known/webfinger" or RemoteUrl has "/ap/actor" or RemoteUrl endswith "trycloudflare.com"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| order by Timestamp desc

NodeBB ActivityPub inbox POST carrying HTML-breakout id (Federation Errors stored XSS) AIExploitation Low
Detects unauthenticated POSTs to the NodeBB ActivityPub inbox where the request body's actor/object 'id' contains HTML metacharacters ( "> < img onerror ). Per the article, inbox.js processes signed Update activities and logs failures into the admin Federation Errors view where {./id} is emitted with auto-escaping disabled (identity function), so a crafted id stores XSS that fires when an administrator opens the log. Requires WAF/proxy request-body capture. Rationale: Anchored on the article's second sink: inbox.js handling signed Update activities and the unescaped {./id} in errors.tpl (Benchpress __escape replaced with identity). Matches POST to the /inbox path plus HTML-breakout tokens in the captured body/query. Confidence Low because it depends on WAF body logging. Cross-checked against: • https://community.nodebb.org/topic/19387/nodebb-v4.14.0-federation-regression-fixes-security-fixes-and-more
ATT&CKT1059.007 T1211
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Benign remote instances whose actor id legitimately contains encoded characters flagged by the naive payload match
  • WAF already normalizing/decoding bodies causing double-encoded false hits
  • Federation debugging tools posting malformed test activities
  • Security scanners fuzzing the ActivityPub inbox
  • Relay software posting large batched activities that trip the substring match
Response runbook
1) Triage: decode the request body and confirm the actor/object 'id' actually contains a script/HTML-breakout ( "><img onerror ) rather than benign encoding; note the source instance and signing key. 2) Contain: if NodeBB <4.14.0, do NOT open the admin Federation Errors page from a privileged browser session (it would fire the stored payload); upgrade first, then purge stored federation error entries. 3) Hunt: check whether any admin/global-moderator account viewed the Federation Errors panel after the malicious POST, and review those sessions and admin actions for XSS-driven account or CSRF-style changes.
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where RequestMethod =~ "POST"
| where RequestURL has "/inbox"
| where Message has_any ("onerror", "<img", "\">", "%22%3E", "&quot;&gt;") or RequestContext has_any ("onerror", "<img", "\">")
| project TimeGenerated, SourceIP, DestinationHostName, RequestURL, RequestMethod, RequestClientApplication, Message
| order by TimeGenerated desc

    where (Processes.process_name IN ("helpers.common.js","inbox.js","render.js","utils.common.js","activitypub.js"))
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name,
       Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_path
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| `security_content_ctime(firstTime)`
| append [
| tstats summariesonly=false count
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("created","modified")
      AND (Filesystem.file_name IN ("helpers.common.js","inbox.js","render.js","utils.common.js","activitypub.js"))
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, Filesystem.process_name,
       Filesystem.file_path, Filesystem.file_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
]

fast16 sabotage implant carrier (svcmgmt.exe) by hash / Lua-carrier behaviour AIInstallation High
Hunts the fast16 host carrier module svcmgmt.exe by its known file hashes and by its distinctive behaviour of running as a service and interpreting embedded Lua 5.0 script arguments. Targets the specific 2005 nuclear-solver sabotage toolkit re-analysed by SentinelLABS, not a generic service binary. Rationale: Anchored on the three SHA256/SHA1/MD5 hashes of the fast16 toolkit plus the carrier binary svcmgmt.exe, whose embedded Lua 5.0 VM and service-carrier behaviour are corroborated by SecurityWeek and the original SentinelLABS fast16 write-up. Hash matches are zero-FP; the svcmgmt.exe+Lua clause adds behavioural coverage for renamed/recompiled variants. Cross-checked against: • https://www.securityweek.com/pre-stuxnet-sabotage-malware-fast16-linked-to-us-iran-cyber-tensions/ • https://cybersecuritynews.com/fast16-malware-with-sabotage-capabilities/ • https://www.sentinelone.com/labs/fast16-mystery-shadowbrokers-reference-reveals-high-precision-software-sabotage-5-years-before-stuxnet/
ATT&CKT1569.002 T1059
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimate third-party or line-of-business service binary coincidentally also named svcmgmt.exe (generic name) triggering the filename clause
  • Security-research or sandbox hosts intentionally detonating the fast16 sample for analysis (e.g. SentinelLABS-style benchmarking)
  • AV/EDR quarantine or IR-collection tooling copying the sample, surfacing the hash in scan telemetry
  • A malware-analyst workstation with the sample in a triage folder that gets executed under a debugger
Suppression filter
| where not (DeviceName has_any ("SANDBOX","MALWARE","IR-") and SHA256 !in~ (fast16_sha256))
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the match is on file hash (definitive) vs the svcmgmt.exe filename clause; check FolderPath, signer, and whether the host is a known malware-analysis/sandbox box. 2) Contain: if hash-confirmed on a production host, network-isolate the endpoint, preserve the binary and any dropped .sys/.lua artifacts, and snapshot memory before the driver can re-patch target software. 3) Hunt: pivot on the same hosts and any that share the file for the fast16.sys driver load (UC2), for high-precision engineering software (LS-DYNA, ANSYS AUTODYN, PKPM, MOHID) present on the box, and for lateral self-propagation of svcmgmt.exe to peers.
let fast16_sha256 = dynamic(["9a10e1faa86a5d39417cae44da5adf38824dfb9a16432e34df766aa1dc9e3525","07c69fc33271cf5a2ce03ac1fed7a3b16357aec093c5bf9ef61fbfa4348d0529","8fcb4d3d4df61719ee3da98241393779290e0efcd88a49e363e2a2dfbc04dae9"]);
let fast16_md5 = dynamic(["dbe51eabebf9d4ef9581ef99844a2944","0ff6abe0252d4f37a196a1231fae5f26","410eddfc19de44249897986ecc8ac449"]);
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(90d)
| where SHA256 in~ (fast16_sha256)
   or MD5 in~ (fast16_md5)
   or (FileName =~ "svcmgmt.exe" and ProcessCommandLine has_any (".lua","lua","fast16"))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256, MD5,
          ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath
| order by Timestamp desc
let fast16_sha256 = dynamic(["9a10e1faa86a5d39417cae44da5adf38824dfb9a16432e34df766aa1dc9e3525","07c69fc33271cf5a2ce03ac1fed7a3b16357aec093c5bf9ef61fbfa4348d0529","8fcb4d3d4df61719ee3da98241393779290e0efcd88a49e363e2a2dfbc04dae9"]);

ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(90d)
| where SHA256 in~ (fast16_sha256)
   or MD5 in~ (fast16_md5)
   or (TargetProcessName has "svcmgmt.exe" and TargetProcessCommandLine has_any (".lua","lua","fast16"))
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetProcessName, SHA256, MD5,
          TargetProcessCommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

fast16 kernel driver (fast16.sys) drop / load — sabotage patching engine AIInstallation High
Hunts for the fast16.sys kernel driver — the component that hosts the 101-rule in-memory patching engine used to tamper with high-precision solver output. Detects the driver being written to disk or loaded, a rare and highly specific artifact of the fast16 sabotage toolkit. Rationale: fast16.sys is the uniquely-named kernel driver corroborated by SecurityWeek and CyberSecurityNews as the fast16 component embedding the in-memory patching engine that tampers with solver calculations; a file/driver by that exact name is effectively pathognomonic for the toolkit with negligible legitimate collision. Cross-checked against: • https://www.securityweek.com/pre-stuxnet-sabotage-malware-fast16-linked-to-us-iran-cyber-tensions/ • https://cybersecuritynews.com/fast16-malware-with-sabotage-capabilities/ • https://www.sentinelone.com/labs/fast16-mystery-shadowbrokers-reference-reveals-high-precision-software-sabotage-5-years-before-stuxnet/
ATT&CKT1014 T1543.003 T1565
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A malware-analysis/reverse-engineering workstation intentionally loading the fast16.sys sample (e.g. the SentinelLABS benchmark environment)
  • An unrelated third-party driver coincidentally named fast16.sys (extremely unlikely given the naming)
  • EDR/AV extracting or quarantining the .sys and re-writing it to a quarantine path, surfacing a FileWritten event
  • IR imaging/collection copying the driver artifact off a compromised host
Suppression filter
| where not (DeviceName has_any ("SANDBOX","MALWARE","RE-LAB"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: verify the FolderPath and the driver's signature/SHA256; determine whether the host is an analysis sandbox or a production/engineering workstation. 2) Contain: on any production host, isolate the endpoint immediately, block driver load via ASR/WDAC where possible, and preserve the .sys plus any Lua scripts and svcmgmt.exe carrier for forensic imaging before the patching engine mutates target-software memory. 3) Hunt: enumerate high-precision solvers on the host (LS-DYNA, ANSYS AUTODYN, PKPM, MOHID) for evidence of in-memory patching or corrupted results, correlate with UC1 svcmgmt.exe execution, and sweep the facility subnet for the same driver name (self-propagation).
DeviceImageLoadEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(90d)
| where FileName =~ "fast16.sys"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| union (
    DeviceFileEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(90d)
    | where FileName =~ "fast16.sys"
    | project Timestamp, DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
              InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
  )
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(90d)
| where TargetFileName =~ "fast16.sys"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName,
          TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, TargetFileSHA256, DvcAction
| order by TimeGenerated desc

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("9a10e1faa86a5d39417cae44da5adf38824dfb9a16432e34df766aa1dc9e3525", "07c69fc33271cf5a2ce03ac1fed7a3b16357aec093c5bf9ef61fbfa4348d0529", "8fcb4d3d4df61719ee3da98241393779290e0efcd88a49e363e2a2dfbc04dae9", "de584703c78a60a56028f9834086facd1401b355", "92e9dcaf7249110047ef121b7586c81d4b8cb4e5", "675cb83cec5f25ebbe8d9f90dea3d836fcb1c234", "dbe51eabebf9d4ef9581ef99844a2944", "0ff6abe0252d4f37a196a1231fae5f26", "410eddfc19de44249897986ecc8ac449") or SHA1 in~ ("9a10e1faa86a5d39417cae44da5adf38824dfb9a16432e34df766aa1dc9e3525", "07c69fc33271cf5a2ce03ac1fed7a3b16357aec093c5bf9ef61fbfa4348d0529", "8fcb4d3d4df61719ee3da98241393779290e0efcd88a49e363e2a2dfbc04dae9", "de584703c78a60a56028f9834086facd1401b355", "92e9dcaf7249110047ef121b7586c81d4b8cb4e5", "675cb83cec5f25ebbe8d9f90dea3d836fcb1c234", "dbe51eabebf9d4ef9581ef99844a2944", "0ff6abe0252d4f37a196a1231fae5f26", "410eddfc19de44249897986ecc8ac449") or MD5 in~ ("9a10e1faa86a5d39417cae44da5adf38824dfb9a16432e34df766aa1dc9e3525", "07c69fc33271cf5a2ce03ac1fed7a3b16357aec093c5bf9ef61fbfa4348d0529", "8fcb4d3d4df61719ee3da98241393779290e0efcd88a49e363e2a2dfbc04dae9", "de584703c78a60a56028f9834086facd1401b355", "92e9dcaf7249110047ef121b7586c81d4b8cb4e5", "675cb83cec5f25ebbe8d9f90dea3d836fcb1c234", "dbe51eabebf9d4ef9581ef99844a2944", "0ff6abe0252d4f37a196a1231fae5f26", "410eddfc19de44249897986ecc8ac449")

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["9a10e1faa86a5d39417cae44da5adf38824dfb9a16432e34df766aa1dc9e3525", "07c69fc33271cf5a2ce03ac1fed7a3b16357aec093c5bf9ef61fbfa4348d0529", "8fcb4d3d4df61719ee3da98241393779290e0efcd88a49e363e2a2dfbc04dae9", "de584703c78a60a56028f9834086facd1401b355", "92e9dcaf7249110047ef121b7586c81d4b8cb4e5", "675cb83cec5f25ebbe8d9f90dea3d836fcb1c234", "dbe51eabebf9d4ef9581ef99844a2944", "0ff6abe0252d4f37a196a1231fae5f26", "410eddfc19de44249897986ecc8ac449"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Real world incident response: Microsoft and AXA XL strengthen cyber resilience

Microsoft Security Blog
2026-07-22 5 use cases 4 techniques 4 kill-chain phases detected

Our collaboration with AXA XL brings Microsoft Incident Response services directly to cyber insurance policyholders, helping organizations coordinate technical, business, and insurance decisions. The post Real world incident response: Microsoft and AXA XL strengthen cyber resilience appeared first on Microsoft Security Blog .

ATT&CKT1190 T1195.002 T1486 T1555.003
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix

Detected
Phase 6
Command & Control
Likely
Phase 7
Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → RMM tool installed by non-IT user — remote-access utility for hands-on-keyboard
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement · Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change · LSASS process access / dump (credential theft)
Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement Actions on Objectives High
psexec / paexec / smbexec / wmic /node — service install over SMB from remote host.
ATT&CKT1021.002 T1569.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
   or (FileName =~ "wmic.exe" and ProcessCommandLine has "/node:")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by Timestamp desc

SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688            // process-create (audit-policy required)
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\psexec.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\psexesvc.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\paexec.exe"
   or (NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe" and CommandLine has "/node:")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: PsExec / SMB Lateral Movement Tooling Execution
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d03
status: experimental
description: |
    PsExec, paexec, smbexec, csexec, remcom — admin-grade tooling that
    pivots over SMB. Pair with `wmic /node:` for the full cluster.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1021/002/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1569/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1021.002
    - attack.t1569.002
    - attack.lateral-movement
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_psexec:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\psexec.exe'
            - '\psexec64.exe'
            - '\psexesvc.exe'
            - '\paexec.exe'
            - '\csexec.exe'
            - '\remcom.exe'
            - '\smbexec.py'
    selection_wmic_remote:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\wmic.exe'
        CommandLine|contains:
            - '/node:'
    condition: selection_psexec or selection_wmic_remote
falsepositives:
    - Sysadmins running approved remote-management tooling — wrap with
      a known-admin-host watchlist
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name IN ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
       OR (Processes.process_name="wmic.exe" AND Processes.process="*/node:*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name IN ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
       OR (Processes.process_name="wmic.exe" AND Processes.process="*/node:*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) Actions on Objectives High
Mimikatz, comsvcs.dll MiniDump, or any non-Windows process opening LSASS.
ATT&CKT1003.001 T1003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceEvents
DeviceEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType == "OpenProcessApiCall"
| where FileName =~ "lsass.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","csrss.exe",
                                          "svchost.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe",
                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent EventID 4663 = object access. AccessMask 0x10 / 0x40 / 0x1010
// indicate read access to lsass.exe — credential-dump signature. Audit
// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4663
| where ObjectName endswith "\lsass.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ObjectName, ProcessName,
          AccessMask, AccessReason
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: LSASS Process Access Outside Microsoft-Signed Tooling
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d04
status: experimental
description: |
    Anything opening lsass.exe with read-memory access that isn't a
    Microsoft signed component (Defender, csrss, etc.) is suspect.
    Mimikatz / Pypykatz / nanodump signature.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1003/001/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1003.001
    - attack.credential-access
logsource:
    category: process_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection:
        TargetImage|endswith: '\lsass.exe'
    filter_known_good:
        SourceImage|endswith:
            - '\MsSense.exe'
            - '\MsMpEng.exe'
            - '\csrss.exe'
            - '\svchost.exe'
            - '\wininit.exe'
            - '\services.exe'
            - '\lsm.exe'
            - '\SearchProtocolHost.exe'
    condition: selection and not filter_known_good
falsepositives:
    - Third-party EDR / forensic tools — extend the filter list
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where (Processes.process="*lsass*" OR Processes.process="*sekurlsa*"
        OR Processes.process="*MiniDump*" OR Processes.process="*comsvcs.dll*MiniDump*"

    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

        OR Processes.process="*MiniDump*" OR Processes.process="*comsvcs.dll*MiniDump*"

SQL injection isn't dead

Aikido
2026-07-22 4 use cases 2 techniques 4 kill-chain phases detected

The fix for SQL injection is decades old and still works. So why did WordPress core just need an emergency patch for one? The data, and how to defend against it. Category: News

CVEsCVE-2026-60137 CVE-2026-63030 CVE-2024-42005
ATT&CKT1021.001 T1190
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix

Reconnaissance

Installation
Detected

Command & Control
Likely
Phase 7
Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] wp2shell SQLi via author__not_in REST parameter (CVE-2026-60137) · [LLM] WordPress web-server/PHP process spawning a shell (wp2shell RCE)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Malicious PHP plugin/webshell dropped in wp-content by web server (wp2shell)
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
wp2shell SQLi via author__not_in REST parameter (CVE-2026-60137) AIExploitation High
Hunts WordPress REST requests where the author__not_in parameter (normally an integer ID array) carries SQL-injection content, the core signature of CVE-2026-60137 exploited through the batch-route desync in the wp2shell chain. Targets the initial unauthenticated exploitation step against /wp-json/wp/v2/posts. Rationale: author__not_in is documented (Rapid7/VulnCheck) to accept only integer author IDs; any SQL metacharacter in it is the CVE-2026-60137 injection signature. Note: PoCs delivered inside a POST /wp-json/batch/v1 body won't appear in access-log query strings — pair with WAF body inspection (CommonSecurityLog RequestURL/DeviceCustomString) to catch batch-wrapped payloads. Defender/Falcon/Datadog/CloudWatch left empty: WordPress web-request telemetry does not live in EDR/CloudTrail. Cross-checked against: • https://www.rapid7.com/blog/post/etr-cve-2026-63030-wp2shell-a-critical-remote-code-execution-vulnerability-in-wordpress-core/ • https://www.tenable.com/blog/wp2shell-cve-2026-63030-cve-2026-60137-frequently-asked-questions-about-remote-code-execution • https://www.vulncheck.com/blog/wp2shell • https://www.darkreading.com/cyberattacks-data-breaches/wp2shell-millions-wordpress-sites-remote-takeover
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesWeb.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Authorized vulnerability scanners (WPScan, Nessus, Qualys) sending SQLi test strings to /wp-json/wp/v2/posts
  • Security researchers / bug-bounty testers replaying the public wp2shell PoC against your own estate
  • Legitimate REST clients that URL-encode a comma-separated author list where an ID happens to contain a matched token (rare)
  • WAF-decoded double requests logging both the encoded and decoded query, doubling alert volume
Suppression filter
| where csUserAgent !has_any ("WPScan","Nessus","Qualys","Nuclei","sqlmap") and cIP !in ("<your_scanner_ips>")
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Pull all requests from the source IP; confirm author__not_in carries SQL (quotes, UNION, SLEEP, information_schema) rather than integers, and note HTTP status — a 200 on a time-based/UNION probe indicates successful injection. 2) CONTAIN: Block the source IP at the WAF/edge and confirm the site is on WordPress 7.0.2 / 6.9.5 / 6.8.6; if unpatched, force the emergency update immediately (WordPress.org enabled forced auto-updates for this). 3) HUNT: Pivot to the same host for post-exploit activity — new administrator accounts, PHP files written under wp-content/plugins or wp-content/uploads, and web-server/PHP processes spawning shells (see companion UCs).
W3CIISLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where csUriStem has "wp-json" or csUriQuery has "rest_route"
| where csUriQuery has "author__not_in"
| where csUriQuery has_any ("'","%27","union","select","sleep","information_schema","0x","--","/*")
| project TimeGenerated, cIP, csMethod, csUriStem, csUriQuery, scStatus, csUserAgent, Computer
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: WordPress wp2shell SQL Injection via author__not_in Parameter (CVE-2026-60137)
id: 8f2c1a44-3e5d-4b6a-9c2f-1a7b9d0e4c11
description: Detects SQL-injection content in the WordPress REST author__not_in parameter, which normally only accepts integer post-author IDs. Core signature of the wp2shell pre-auth RCE chain.
references:
  - https://www.rapid7.com/blog/post/etr-cve-2026-63030-wp2shell-a-critical-remote-code-execution-vulnerability-in-wordpress-core/
  - https://www.tenable.com/blog/wp2shell-cve-2026-63030-cve-2026-60137-frequently-asked-questions-about-remote-code-execution
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/07/22
tags:
  - attack.t1190
  - attack.initial_access
logsource:
  category: webserver
detection:
  selection_param:
    cs-uri-query|contains: 'author__not_in'
  selection_sqli:
    cs-uri-query|contains:
      - "'"
      - '%27'
      - 'union'
      - 'select'
      - 'sleep'
      - 'information_schema'
      - '0x'
      - '--'
      - '/*'
  condition: selection_param and selection_sqli
falsepositives:
  - Security scanners (Nessus, Qualys, WPScan) probing the endpoint
level: high
CLOUD
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime values(Web.status) as status values(Web.http_method) as http_method from datamodel=Web where Web.url="*author__not_in*" AND (Web.url="*'*" OR Web.url="*%27*" OR Web.url="*union*" OR Web.url="*UNION*" OR Web.url="*select*" OR Web.url="*SELECT*" OR Web.url="*sleep*" OR Web.url="*information_schema*" OR Web.url="*0x*" OR Web.url="*--*" OR Web.url="*/*") by Web.src Web.dest Web.http_user_agent Web.url | `drop_dm_object_name(Web)` | sort - lastTime
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime values(Web.status) as status values(Web.http_method) as http_method from datamodel=Web where Web.url="*author__not_in*" AND (Web.url="*'*" OR Web.url="*%27*" OR Web.url="*union*" OR Web.url="*UNION*" OR Web.url="*select*" OR Web.url="*SELECT*" OR Web.url="*sleep*" OR Web.url="*information_schema*" OR Web.url="*0x*" OR Web.url="*--*" OR Web.url="*/*") by Web.src Web.dest Web.http_user_agent Web.url | `drop_dm_object_name(Web)` | sort - lastTime
WordPress web-server/PHP process spawning a shell (wp2shell RCE) AIExploitation High
Detects the code-execution stage of wp2shell where the web-request handler (php-fpm, php-cgi, w3wp, apache/nginx) spawns a command shell or download utility — behaviour a normal WordPress worker never exhibits. Confirms the SQLi chain has reached remote code execution. Rationale: The wp2shell chain culminates in uploading a malicious plugin for code execution; that code runs under the PHP/web-server process, so a web worker spawning cmd/sh/whoami/curl is a direct RCE confirmation. Datadog uses windows.sysmon (IIS/PHP-on-Windows); Linux hosts should use source:linux.auditd EXECVE with the same process names. Cross-checked against: • https://www.rapid7.com/blog/post/etr-cve-2026-63030-wp2shell-a-critical-remote-code-execution-vulnerability-in-wordpress-core/ • https://cyberinsider.com/wordpress-releases-emergency-update-for-critical-wp2shell-rce-flaw/ • https://www.indusface.com/blog/wp2shell-wordpress-cve-2026-60137-63030-rce/
ATT&CKT1505.003 T1059
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Backup plugins (UpdraftPlus, BackWPup) shelling out to mysqldump/gzip/tar
  • Image-optimisation plugins invoking imagemagick 'convert' or 'jpegoptim' via PHP exec()
  • wp-cron running PHP CLI jobs that legitimately call curl to fetch feed data
  • Server-monitoring agents that fork sh from the PHP-FPM pool for health checks
  • Composer/WP-CLI maintenance run interactively on the web host by an admin
Suppression filter
| where not (FileName in~ ("sh","bash") and ProcessCommandLine has_any ("mysqldump","imagemagick","convert","jpegoptim","wp-cron.php","backwpup","updraft"))
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Inspect the child ProcessCommandLine — reconnaissance (whoami, id, uname), outbound fetch (curl/wget to an unknown host), or an interactive shell are strong TP signals; confirm the parent PHP worker is serving the WordPress vhost. 2) CONTAIN: Isolate the host, kill the offending process tree, and rotate WordPress DB and admin credentials; take a forensic copy of wp-content before remediation. 3) HUNT: Enumerate all administrator accounts for rogue additions, list recently-modified PHP files under wp-content/plugins and wp-content/uploads, and review web logs for the preceding author__not_in / batch/v1 exploitation requests.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("php-cgi.exe","php.exe","php-fpm","w3wp.exe","httpd.exe","httpd","apache2","nginx.exe","nginx")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","sh","bash","dash","whoami.exe","whoami","curl.exe","curl","wget","python.exe","python3")
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("php-cgi","php.exe","php-fpm","w3wp","httpd","apache2","nginx")
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("cmd.exe","powershell","pwsh","/sh","/bash","/dash","whoami","curl","wget","python")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: WordPress Web-Server/PHP Process Spawning Shell (wp2shell RCE)
id: 2b9e5c17-6d41-4a8e-b3c0-7f21a9e4d502
description: Web-request handler (php-fpm/php-cgi/w3wp/apache/nginx) spawning a command interpreter or download tool, indicating web-shell command execution from the wp2shell chain.
references:
  - https://www.rapid7.com/blog/post/etr-cve-2026-63030-wp2shell-a-critical-remote-code-execution-vulnerability-in-wordpress-core/
  - https://cyberinsider.com/wordpress-releases-emergency-update-for-critical-wp2shell-rce-flaw/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/07/22

  - attack.t1505.003
  - attack.t1059
  - attack.execution

  category: process_creation
detection:
  selection:
    ParentImage|endswith:
      - '\php-cgi.exe'
      - '\php.exe'
      - '\w3wp.exe'

      - '/apache2'
      - '/httpd'
      - '/nginx'
    Image|endswith:
      - '\cmd.exe'
      - '\powershell.exe'
      - '\pwsh.exe'
      - '/sh'
      - '/bash'
      - '/dash'
      - '\whoami.exe'
      - '/whoami'
      - '/curl'
      - '/wget'
      - '/python3'
  condition: selection
falsepositives:
  - Backup/media plugins invoking imagemagick, mysqldump or curl via shell
level: high
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 @ParentImage:(*\\php-cgi.exe OR *\\php.exe OR *\\w3wp.exe OR *\\W3WP.exe) @Image:(*\\cmd.exe OR *\\powershell.exe OR *\\PowerShell.exe OR *\\pwsh.exe)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2
| ParentBaseFileName=/^(php-cgi|php|php-fpm|w3wp|httpd|apache2|nginx)(\.exe)?$/i
| ImageFileName=/(cmd|powershell|pwsh|sh|bash|dash|whoami|curl|wget|python3?)(\.exe)?$/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ParentBaseFileName, ImageFileName, CommandLine], function=count())

Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where (Processes.parent_process_name IN ("php-cgi.exe","php.exe","php-fpm","w3wp.exe","httpd","httpd.exe","apache2","nginx","nginx.exe")) AND (Processes.process_name IN ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","sh","bash","dash","whoami","whoami.exe","curl","curl.exe","wget","python","python3")) by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.parent_process_name Processes.process_name Processes.process | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | sort - lastTime

Malicious PHP plugin/webshell dropped in wp-content by web server (wp2shell) AIInstallation Medium
Hunts PHP files written into wp-content/plugins or wp-content/uploads by the web-server/PHP process — the persistence/execution payload the wp2shell chain uploads after minting a rogue admin. Distinguishes attacker-dropped web shells from normal user-initiated plugin installs by anchoring on the writing process and target path. Rationale: The documented wp2shell escalation uploads a malicious plugin for code execution; a PHP file written into wp-content/plugins or the uploads dir by the PHP/web-server process is the on-disk artefact of that step. Anchored on both the target path and writing process to separate attacker drops from normal user-driven plugin installs. Cross-checked against: • https://www.rapid7.com/blog/post/etr-cve-2026-63030-wp2shell-a-critical-remote-code-execution-vulnerability-in-wordpress-core/ • https://www.vulncheck.com/blog/wp2shell • https://www.tenable.com/blog/wp2shell-cve-2026-63030-cve-2026-60137-frequently-asked-questions-about-remote-code-execution
ATT&CKT1505.003 T1505
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Administrator installing or updating a legitimate plugin from the WordPress dashboard (writes many .php files to wp-content/plugins)
  • Automatic plugin/theme auto-updates writing PHP during maintenance windows
  • Caching plugins (W3 Total Cache, WP Rocket) generating advanced-cache.php or object-cache.php
  • Media/gallery plugins writing helper PHP into wp-content/uploads
  • WP-CLI 'wp plugin install' run by an operator on the host
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("wp plugin install","wp plugin update","advanced-cache.php","object-cache.php") or FolderPath has_any ("w3-total-cache","wp-rocket"))
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Read the dropped PHP — short obfuscated files or those containing eval/base64_decode/system/assert/$_POST[ are web shells; a single .php landing directly in wp-content/uploads is almost never legitimate. Check whether the write coincides with a dashboard plugin install by a known admin. 2) CONTAIN: Quarantine the file, hash and submit it, and isolate the host if it is a web shell; revoke sessions and reset admin/DB credentials. 3) HUNT: Correlate the write time backward to author__not_in/batch/v1 exploitation in web logs and forward to web-server processes spawning shells or outbound C2, and audit wp_users for a rogue administrator created just before the drop.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")
| where FolderPath has "wp-content" and (FolderPath has "plugins" or FolderPath has "uploads")
| where FileName endswith ".php"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("php-cgi.exe","php.exe","php-fpm","w3wp.exe","httpd.exe","httpd","nginx.exe","nginx","apache2")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where TargetFilePath has "wp-content" and (TargetFilePath has "plugins" or TargetFilePath has "uploads")
| where TargetFileName endswith ".php"
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("php-cgi","php.exe","php-fpm","w3wp","httpd","apache2","nginx")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, TargetFileSHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Malicious PHP Written to WordPress wp-content by Web Server (wp2shell)

The upgrade trap: when upgrading is the wrong answer to a CVE

Aikido
2026-07-22 5 use cases 1 technique 5 kill-chain phases detected

Upgrading to fix a CVE sounds straightforward. But the patched version often breaks your app, hasn't shipped yet, or doesn't exist. Here's why, and what actually works. Category: News

CVEsCVE-2026-48937
ATT&CKT1190
Domainsnpmjs.help
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix
Phase 1
Reconnaissance
Detected
Phase 2
Weaponization
—
Phase 3
Delivery
Detected
Phase 4
Exploitation
Detected
Phase 5
Installation
Likely
Phase 6
Command & Control
Detected
Phase 7
Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Inbound phishing email or URL referencing npm look-alike domain npmjs.help · [LLM] Endpoint DNS resolution or web connection to npm phishing domain npmjs.help
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Inbound phishing email or URL referencing npm look-alike domain npmjs.help AIDelivery High
Hunts inbound mail whose sender domain or embedded URL uses the typosquat domain npmjs.help, the exact lure infrastructure used to phish the npm maintainer of chalk/debug and hijack their account. Any mail touching this domain is credential-harvest phishing, not a legitimate npm communication (real npm uses npmjs.com / github.com). Rationale: Anchored on the exact IOC npmjs.help (sender domain and embedded URL), the phishing infrastructure confirmed by Sygnia/StepSecurity/Semgrep as the vector that compromised the chalk & debug maintainer account. Sentinel left blank: this tenant's provided schema exposes no Microsoft Defender for Office 365 email tables (EmailEvents/EmailUrlInfo have no clean Sentinel equivalent; OfficeActivity does not carry sender/URL domain reliably). Datadog/Falcon/CloudWatch blank: FDR and these sources do not ship M365 mailflow telemetry. Cross-checked against: • https://www.sygnia.co/threat-reports-and-advisories/npm-supply-chain-attack-september-2025/ • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/20-popular-npm-packages-compromised-chalk-debug-strip-ansi-color-convert-wrap-ansi • https://semgrep.dev/blog/2025/chalk-debug-and-color-on-npm-compromised-in-new-supply-chain-attack/
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1598.003
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Internal purple-team / phishing-simulation platform deliberately spoofing npmjs.help to test user awareness
  • A user forwarding the phishing message to the SOC or a phishing-report mailbox (original npmjs.help URL still present in EmailUrlInfo)
  • Security-newsletter or threat-intel digest whose body links out to npmjs.help as a documented IOC (matches on EmailUrl but is benign reporting)
  • Managed-detection vendor sending an advisory that embeds the IOC domain
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: pull every recipient of mail matching npmjs.help via NetworkMessageId; determine DeliveryAction (Delivered vs Blocked) and whether any recipient is an npm/GitHub package maintainer or CI/CD service account. 2) CONTAIN: soft-delete/quarantine the message across all mailboxes (Explorer purge), block npmjs.help at mail gateway and web proxy, and force credential reset + re-registration of MFA for any recipient who opened the link. 3) HUNT: pivot to UrlClickEvents and DeviceNetworkEvents for npmjs.help to find users who actually reached the portal; audit npm/GitHub account activity (new tokens, published package versions) for any maintainer recipient.
let lookback = 90d;
union
(EmailEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(lookback)
| where SenderFromDomain =~ "npmjs.help" or SenderMailFromDomain =~ "npmjs.help"
| project Timestamp, NetworkMessageId, SenderFromAddress, RecipientEmailAddress, Subject, DeliveryAction, DeliveryLocation, Indicator = SenderFromDomain, Source = "SenderDomain"),
(EmailUrlInfo
| where Timestamp > ago(lookback)
| where UrlDomain =~ "npmjs.help"
| project Timestamp, NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain, Indicator = UrlDomain, Source = "EmailUrl")
| order by Timestamp desc
// Microsoft Sentinel — auto-derived from the Defender XDR hunt.
// Requires the Microsoft 365 Defender data connector (streams the
// advanced-hunting tables into Sentinel; Timestamp → TimeGenerated).
let lookback = 90d;
union
(EmailEvents
| where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback)
| where SenderFromDomain =~ "npmjs.help" or SenderMailFromDomain =~ "npmjs.help"
| project TimeGenerated, NetworkMessageId, SenderFromAddress, RecipientEmailAddress, Subject, DeliveryAction, DeliveryLocation, Indicator = SenderFromDomain, Source = "SenderDomain"),
(EmailUrlInfo
| where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback)
| where UrlDomain =~ "npmjs.help"
| project TimeGenerated, NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain, Indicator = UrlDomain, Source = "EmailUrl")
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Endpoint DNS resolution or web connection to npm phishing domain npmjs.help AIDelivery High
Detects any host resolving or connecting to npmjs.help, the credential-harvest portal impersonating npm support in the September 2025 chalk/debug supply-chain compromise. Reaching this domain means a user clicked the lure and hit the fake 2FA/login page; there is no legitimate reason for an enterprise host to contact it. Rationale: Single hard IOC (npmjs.help) confirmed by multiple second sources as the phishing portal that harvested the maintainer's npm credentials; token-aligned domain match on network/DNS telemetry is near-zero-FP because the domain has no legitimate business use. Sysmon field DestinationHostname enumerated in two casings per Datadog case-sensitivity; Falcon DomainName is lower-cased but regex /i keeps it robust. Cross-checked against: • https://www.sygnia.co/threat-reports-and-advisories/npm-supply-chain-attack-september-2025/ • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/20-popular-npm-packages-compromised-chalk-debug-strip-ansi-color-convert-wrap-ansi • https://vercel.com/blog/critical-npm-supply-chain-attack-response-september-8-2025
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1598.003
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution.DNS
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • SOC analyst or threat researcher manually browsing npmjs.help during IOC investigation from a corporate host
  • Malware-analysis sandbox / detonation VM resolving the domain as part of automated URL detonation
  • Threat-intel enrichment tooling (TIP, VirusTotal relay) performing passive DNS lookups of the IOC
  • DNS sinkhole or protective-DNS solution logging the query as it blocks it (still worth confirming which host asked)
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: identify the host/user that initiated the connection (InitiatingProcessFileName should be a browser); confirm it is not a designated analyst/sandbox host, and check whether the request succeeded (ActionType=ConnectionSuccess vs a blocked/sinkholed response). 2) CONTAIN: isolate the host if a browser reached the live portal, block npmjs.help network-wide, and force password + MFA re-enrollment for the user, revoking any npm/GitHub personal-access tokens they hold. 3) HUNT: correlate back to EmailEvents/UrlClickEvents for npmjs.help to scope all recipients, and audit the org's npm dependency lockfiles/CI logs for installation of the compromised chalk/debug/ansi-styles versions pulled during the exposure window.

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc

| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

2026-07-21 3 use cases 1 technique 2 kill-chain phases detected

Gitea: Public-only repository tokens can update private PR head branches

CVEsCVE-2026-58443
ATT&CKT1190

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).

Gitea PR head-branch update via API v1 pulls/{index}/update (CVE-2026-58443 exploit vector) AIExploitation Medium
Surfaces POST requests to the Gitea REST endpoint POST /api/v1/repos/{owner}/{repo}/pulls/{index}/update, the exact route abused by CVE-2026-58443 where a public-only,write:repository token forces a server-side push into a PRIVATE head branch through a PUBLIC base repo. Programmatic API calls to this route are low-volume (the UI 'Update branch' button uses the web route, not /api/v1/), so hunting API-path hits and triaging the requesting token's scope is the cleanest available discriminator. Rationale: Uses the exact vulnerable route from the advisory (POST /api/v1/repos/{owner}/{repo}/pulls/{index}/update). Distinguished from the UI 'Update branch' feature by scoping to the /api/v1/ programmatic path. Defender KQL left empty: Advanced Hunting has no table for inbound web-app request paths; this must run on proxy/WAF/web-access telemetry. Cross-checked GitHub advisory GHSA-xxjv-752h-3vp2 and secondary write-ups (securityonline, gbhackers) confirming the endpoint and CVSS 9.6. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-xxjv-752h-3vp2 • https://github.com/go-gitea/gitea/security/advisories/GHSA-xxjv-752h-3vp2 • https://securityonline.info/gitea-vulnerability/ • https://gbhackers.com/critical-gitea-flaw-trigger-actions-workflows/
ATT&CKT1068 T1550.001 T1078.004 T1190
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate CI/CD automation calling the PR update API to keep feature branches current with base
  • Developers or bots using the Gitea REST API (Tea CLI, custom scripts) to update PRs on repos they legitimately own
  • Renovate/Dependabot-style bots invoking pulls/update programmatically
  • Mirror/sync tooling that updates PR head branches via API on private repos it is authorized for
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: For each hit, pull the requesting access token from Gitea (admin API /api/v1/admin or DB) and confirm its scope — a public-only,write:repository token hitting this route against a public base repo whose PR head repo is PRIVATE is the exploit signature; note source IP/UA and the PR index. 2) CONTAIN: Revoke the offending token, and upgrade Gitea from any ≤1.26.4 build to 1.27.0 (the fixed release). 3) HUNT: Review the affected private repo's git reflog/push events for server-side pushes attributed to the PR-update trigger, and check whether any Gitea Actions push-triggered workflows fired in private repos as a downstream effect of the forced push.
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where RequestMethod == "POST"
| where RequestURL matches regex @"(?i)/api/v1/repos/[^/]+/[^/]+/pulls/[0-9]+/update"
| project TimeGenerated, SourceIP, SourceUserName, DestinationHostName, RequestURL, RequestMethod, DeviceAction, RequestClientApplication, DeviceVendor, DeviceProduct
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Vulnerable Gitea instance exposed to CVE-2026-58443 (≤1.26.4) AIExploitation High
Exposure hunt that flags managed devices running a Gitea version vulnerable to CVE-2026-58443 (all builds up to and including 1.26.4; fixed in 1.27.0). Prioritises patching of internet-facing or shared self-hosted Gitea servers before the public PoC is weaponised. Rationale: Concrete CVE id (CVE-2026-58443) and fixed version (1.27.0) confirmed via the GitHub advisory and Gitea security advisory; vulnerable range ≤1.26.4. TVM CveId match is high-fidelity and zero-cmdline-noise. Datadog/Falcon/CloudWatch left empty — Gitea version inventory is not shipped to those platforms in this tenant. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-xxjv-752h-3vp2 • https://advisories.gitlab.com/golang/code.gitea.io/gitea/CVE-2026-58443/ • https://securityonline.info/gitea-vulnerability/
ATT&CKT1190 T1068

Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment

  • TVM version parsing lag immediately after an upgrade to 1.27.0 shows a stale vulnerable state
  • Dev/test Gitea containers spun up transiently that TVM inventories before teardown
