Gitea Actions V4 artifact signed-URL forgery via oversized artifactID (CVE-2026-58426) AIExploitation High
Hunts DownloadArtifact/UploadArtifact requests whose artifactID query parameter carries an abnormally long digit run — the digit-concatenation fingerprint of the CVE-2026-58426 HMAC boundary-shift collision, where digits from taskID/artifactName are shifted into artifactID to preserve the original signature. Legit Gitea artifact DB IDs are small sequential int64 values; a 12+ digit artifactID is the forged tuple. Rationale: Uses the article's exact endpoint names (DownloadArtifact/UploadArtifact) and the artifactID query parameter, plus the documented collision mechanic where the forged artifactID = shifted-digits + real ID (e.g. '48<attacker id>'), producing an int64 far longer than any real sequential Gitea artifact DB ID. Single-event, high fidelity. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-hg5r-vq93-9fv6 • https://advisories.gitlab.com/golang/code.gitea.io/gitea/CVE-2026-58426/ • https://github.com/go-gitea/gitea/pull/37707 • https://www.thehackerwire.com/gitea-actions-artifacts-hmac-ambiguity-allows-cross-repository-access/
ATT&CKT1550.001 T1213
Data sourcesWeb.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Load/stress testing tools replaying artifact URLs with synthetic IDs
  • Custom CI tooling that appends tracking digits to artifact identifiers
  • Log-replay or SIEM backfill duplicating historical requests
  • A Gitea fork that changed artifactID encoding to include run/attempt prefixes
  • Security scanners (e.g. internal DAST) probing the artifact endpoints post-patch
Suppression filter
| where NOT (cs-uri-query matches "loadtest" OR src IN ("<internal_scanner_ip_ranges_via_lookup>"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm Gitea version — if <1.26.2 the instance is vulnerable (CVE-2026-58426). Pull the full request: note SourceIP, the artifactName, taskID and the 12+ digit artifactID, and HTTP status (200 = served/accepted). 2) Contain: upgrade Gitea to 1.26.2+ immediately; rotate GetGeneralTokenSigningSecret so any captured signed URLs are invalidated; restrict who can run Actions on private repos. 3) Hunt: for the same SourceIP, pivot to UC2 (sig reuse) and UC3 (cross-task download fan-out) to determine which private artifacts were read or which task staging areas were written.
// Point at the reverse-proxy/WAF CEF feed fronting Gitea. Swap to W3CIISLog(csUriQuery)/AppServiceHTTPLogs(CsUriQuery) if that is the proxy.
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where RequestURL has_any ("DownloadArtifact","UploadArtifact")
| where RequestURL has "artifactID="
| extend artifactID = extract(@"artifactID=(\d+)", 1, RequestURL)
| where isnotempty(artifactID) and strlen(artifactID) >= 12   // 12+ digits = forged concatenated int64, real Gitea IDs are small sequential
| project TimeGenerated, SourceIP, DestinationHostName, RequestMethod, RequestURL, artifactID, ArtifactIDLen = strlen(artifactID)
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Gitea Actions V4 Artifact Signed URL Forgery via Oversized artifactID (CVE-2026-58426)
id: 6f3b1c2e-9a44-4d1e-b8c7-2f0a5e7d1a90
status: experimental
description: Detects Gitea Actions Artifacts V4 DownloadArtifact/UploadArtifact requests whose artifactID parameter carries an abnormally long digit run, the signature-payload boundary-shift fingerprint of CVE-2026-58426.
references:
  - https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-hg5r-vq93-9fv6
  - https://github.com/go-gitea/gitea/pull/37707
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/08/04
tags:
  - attack.t1550.001
  - attack.defense_evasion
logsource:
  category: webserver
detection:
  selection_endpoint:
    cs-uri-stem|contains:
      - 'DownloadArtifact'
      - 'UploadArtifact'
  selection_forged:
    cs-uri-query|re: 'artifactID=\d{12,}'
  condition: selection_endpoint and selection_forged
falsepositives:
  - Extremely large Gitea instances that have genuinely issued 12+ digit artifact IDs (implausible without billions of artifacts)
level: high
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Web where (Web.url="*DownloadArtifact*" OR Web.url="*UploadArtifact*") Web.uri_query="*artifactID=*" by Web.src, Web.dest, Web.http_method, Web.status, Web.uri_query
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rex field=uri_query "artifactID=(?<artifactID>\d+)"
| eval artifactID_len=len(artifactID)
| where artifactID_len>=12
| convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| sort - lastTime
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Web where (Web.url="*DownloadArtifact*" OR Web.url="*UploadArtifact*") Web.uri_query="*artifactID=*" by Web.src, Web.dest, Web.http_method, Web.status, Web.uri_query
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rex field=uri_query "artifactID=(?<artifactID>\d+)"
| eval artifactID_len=len(artifactID)
| where artifactID_len>=12
| convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| sort - lastTime
Gitea artifact HMAC signature reused across mismatched task/artifact tuples (CVE-2026-58426) AIExploitation High
Detects a single HMAC sig value appearing on artifact requests with two or more distinct (artifactName, taskID, artifactID) tuples. Because the vulnerable signature payload is ambiguous, one valid signature can be presented against a rewritten URL context — a signature that binds to more than one tuple is direct evidence of the CVE-2026-58426 boundary-shift collision being exploited. Rationale: Directly models the root cause from the article: the HMAC is built from raw concatenated fields so two different (artifactName, taskID, artifactID) tuples yield the same sig. Observing one sig against >1 tuple is a near-zero-FP exploitation signal using the article's exact query parameters. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-hg5r-vq93-9fv6 • https://advisories.gitlab.com/golang/code.gitea.io/gitea/CVE-2026-58426/ • https://github.com/go-gitea/gitea/pull/37707
ATT&CKT1550.001 T1539
Data sourcesWeb.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Client retry logic that re-requests the identical signed URL (same tuple — excluded by the >1 distinct-tuple gate)
  • Proxy logging the URL twice with URL-encoding differences making artifactName appear distinct
  • A Gitea proxy that rewrites/normalizes query-parameter order and duplicates events
  • SIEM ingesting the same access log from two proxy tiers
  • Automated pen-test of the CVE by the internal red team
Suppression filter
| where distinct_tuples > 1
Response runbook
1) Triage: for the flagged sig, list every distinct tuple and the SourceIP(s). The tuple set reveals which victim task/artifact contexts were targeted vs the attacker's own signed artifact. Check HTTP status to see which succeeded. 2) Contain: upgrade to Gitea 1.26.2+ and rotate the general token signing secret to invalidate all outstanding signed URLs; suspend the offending runner/user. 3) Hunt: enumerate the victim taskIDs to identify affected private repositories; if any UploadArtifact tuples are present, treat downstream FinalizeArtifact output as potentially tampered and review those workflow runs.
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where RequestURL has_any ("DownloadArtifact","UploadArtifact")
| where RequestURL has "sig="
| extend sig = extract(@"sig=([^&]+)", 1, RequestURL),
         artifactName = extract(@"artifactName=([^&]+)", 1, RequestURL),
         taskID = extract(@"taskID=(\d+)", 1, RequestURL),
         artifactID = extract(@"artifactID=(\d+)", 1, RequestURL)
| where isnotempty(sig)
| extend tuple = strcat(artifactName, "|", taskID, "|", artifactID)
| summarize DistinctTuples = dcount(tuple), Tuples = make_set(tuple, 20), Sources = make_set(SourceIP, 20), Requests = count(), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated) by sig
| where DistinctTuples > 1   // a valid HMAC must bind to exactly one tuple; >1 = boundary-shift collision
| order by DistinctTuples desc

Gitea runner enumerating cross-task DownloadArtifact contexts (CVE-2026-58426 read path) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Detects one source issuing DownloadArtifact requests against many distinct taskID contexts in a short window — the cross-task/cross-repository private artifact read primitive (C:H) of CVE-2026-58426. A legitimate runner downloads artifacts for its own task; fan-out across unrelated task IDs from one source is enumeration of other tasks' private artifacts. Rationale: The article states the download path independently demonstrates cross-repository private artifact disclosure and that the target task must be running. Fan-out across many taskID values (article's exact parameter) from a single source is the observable footprint of that enumeration; threshold is tunable per CI concurrency. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-hg5r-vq93-9fv6 • https://advisories.gitlab.com/golang/code.gitea.io/gitea/CVE-2026-58426/ • https://www.mallory.ai/stories/019f2b51-04a6-79ce-8770-2ba9a917d861
ATT&CKT1213 T1550.001
Data sourcesWeb.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A matrix/monorepo workflow whose single runner legitimately fans out downloads across many child job tasks
  • Artifact caching proxies or mirrors that pre-fetch multiple tasks' artifacts
  • A shared NAT egress IP collapsing many distinct runners into one SourceIP
  • Backup/archival tooling bulk-downloading artifacts
  • CI dashboards polling artifact availability across tasks
Suppression filter
| where NOT (src IN ("<known_nat_egress_ips_via_lookup>") OR src IN ("<artifact_cache_proxy_ips_via_lookup>"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm whether the SourceIP maps to a single runner or a NAT gateway. If a single runner, the taskID set spanning unrelated repos is the tell — map each taskID to its repository. 2) Contain: on Gitea <1.26.2, upgrade and rotate the token signing secret; isolate the runner and revoke its registration token. 3) Hunt: correlate the same SourceIP against UC1 (oversized artifactID) and UC2 (sig reuse) to confirm forgery vs benign fan-out, and inventory which private-repo artifacts were served (status 200).

| order by DistinctTasks desc

Gitea artifact staging write into a task context from multiple sources (CVE-2026-58426 write path) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Detects a single (taskID, artifactName) upload-staging target receiving UploadArtifact PUT requests from more than one distinct source. Legitimate V4 artifact uploads for a task come from that task's single runner; a second source writing into the same task/artifact staging context is the cross-task upload-state write (I:H) primitive of CVE-2026-58426, whose bytes FinalizeArtifact later consumes. Rationale: Maps the article's UploadArtifact write primitive: the forged URL resolves the target artifact by the URL-selected task/run/name and appends attacker bytes. Two distinct sources writing the same (taskID, artifactName) staging target is the observable fingerprint of a foreign context writing into a victim task, using the article's exact PUT endpoint and parameters. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-hg5r-vq93-9fv6 • https://advisories.gitlab.com/golang/code.gitea.io/gitea/CVE-2026-58426/ • https://www.thehackerwire.com/gitea-actions-artifacts-hmac-ambiguity-allows-cross-repository-access/
ATT&CKT1565.001
Data sourcesWeb.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Chunked/resumable uploads where a runner's egress IP changes mid-transfer (rare, DHCP/NAT re-map)
  • A runner behind a load-balanced NAT pool presenting rotating source IPs
  • Retried uploads after a runner restart on a different host
  • Proxy tiers rewriting SourceIP inconsistently (missing X-Forwarded-For normalization)
  • Multi-region runner failover re-uploading the same artifact
Suppression filter
| where NOT (target IN ("<resumable_upload_targets_via_lookup>"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: for the flagged (taskID, artifactName) target, list the source IPs and their timestamps; determine which IP is the task's legitimate runner (cross-ref runner registration) and which is foreign. Check whether X-Forwarded-For normalization is masking the true client. 2) Contain: on Gitea <1.26.2, upgrade and rotate the token signing secret; quarantine any workflow run whose artifact was written by a foreign source and block its FinalizeArtifact output from deployment. 3) Hunt: pivot the foreign SourceIP through UC1/UC2 to confirm signature forgery, and audit downstream jobs/releases that consumed the tampered artifact.

| order by DistinctSources desc

Gitea Docker image: `REVERSE_PROXY_TRUSTED_PROXIES = *` default lets any source IP impersonate any user via `X-WEBAUTH-USER`

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Client-supplied X-WEBAUTH-USER header reaching Gitea (CVE-2026-20896 impersonation)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Gitea container started with reverse-proxy auth exposing permissive trusted-proxies (CVE-2026-20896)
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Privileged Gitea action correlated with client-supplied X-WEBAUTH-USER (CVE-2026-20896)
Client-supplied X-WEBAUTH-USER header reaching Gitea (CVE-2026-20896 impersonation) AIExploitation High
Hunts requests carrying an X-WEBAUTH-USER header from a source IP outside the documented trusted-proxy scope (loopback 127.0.0.0/8, ::1), or any direct connection to the Gitea container port that bypasses the intended authenticating reverse proxy. This is the raw exploitation primitive of CVE-2026-20896 where the vulnerable Docker image default REVERSE_PROXY_TRUSTED_PROXIES=* trusts the header from any source, letting an attacker be authenticated as any user with no password or cookie. Rationale: Uses the exact exploit header string 'X-WEBAUTH-USER' and the documented safe-default trust scope (127.0.0.0/8, ::1) from the advisory: any non-loopback source presenting the header, or any direct connection to Gitea's default port 3000 bypassing the proxy, is the CVE-2026-20896 primitive. Corroborated by BleepingComputer and the public PoC checker. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-f75j-4cw6-rmx4 • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/hackers-exploit-critical-auth-bypass-in-gitea-docker-image/ • https://github.com/szybnev/cve-2026-20896-gitea-poc • https://thehackernews.com/2026/07/threat-actors-probe-gitea-docker-flaw.html
ATT&CKT1556 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Network_Traffic Web.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • The legitimate authenticating reverse proxy itself injecting X-WEBAUTH-USER from an internal/known appliance IP (should be allow-listed)
  • Health-check or monitoring tooling that reaches port 3000 directly without going through the proxy
  • Load balancer/service mesh sidecar terminating on a non-loopback interface
  • Security scanners (Nessus/Qualys) sending the header during authenticated-bypass testing
  • Migration/replication jobs that connect container-to-container
Suppression filter
| where RemoteIP != "<insert your reverse-proxy appliance IP>"  // supply your proxy IP to suppress the legitimate injector
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Confirm the source IP is NOT your authenticating reverse-proxy appliance; check whether the impersonated username in the header is a privileged account (admin, gitea_admin). Correlate with Gitea audit events in the same window. Step 2 (contain): Set REVERSE_PROXY_TRUSTED_PROXIES to 127.0.0.0/8,::1/128 (or the real proxy CIDR) and restart; upgrade the image to >=1.26.3; block direct external reach to port 3000 at the network layer. Step 3 (hunt): Pivot on the impersonated usernames for token creation, SSH-key additions, repo clones, and secret/webhook reads performed by those accounts during the exposure window.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(24h)
| where ActionType == "InboundConnectionAccepted"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName has "gitea"
| where LocalPort == 3000
| where RemoteIP !startswith "127." and RemoteIP != "::1"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, LocalPort, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
Syslog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(24h)
| where ProcessName in~ ("nginx","apache2","httpd","gitea","gitea-web")
| where SyslogMessage has "x-webauth-user"
| extend webauth_user = extract("(?i)x-webauth-user:? *([^ ]+)", 1, SyslogMessage)
| extend clientip = extract("([0-9]+\\.[0-9]+\\.[0-9]+\\.[0-9]+)", 1, SyslogMessage)
| where isnotempty(webauth_user)
| where isnotempty(clientip) and clientip !startswith "127." and clientip != "::1"
| summarize hits=count(), firstseen=min(TimeGenerated), lastseen=max(TimeGenerated), users=make_set(webauth_user, 20) by clientip, ProcessName, Computer
| order by hits desc
#event_simpleName=NetworkConnectIP4
| ConnectionDirection=1
| LocalPort=3000
| ContextImageFileName=/gitea/i
| NOT cidr("127.0.0.0/8", field=RemoteAddressIP4)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, RemoteAddressIP4, ContextImageFileName], function=count())
WEB
index=web (sourcetype=nginx:plus:access OR sourcetype=apache:access OR sourcetype=gitea)
| search _raw="*x-webauth-user*"
| rex field=_raw "(?i)x-webauth-user:? *(?<webauth_user>[^ ]+)"
| rex field=_raw "(?<client_ip>[0-9]+\.[0-9]+\.[0-9]+\.[0-9]+)"
| where isnotnull(webauth_user) AND NOT cidrmatch("127.0.0.0/8", client_ip)
| stats count min(_time) AS firstTime max(_time) AS lastTime values(webauth_user) AS impersonated_users by client_ip, host, sourcetype
| sort - count
Privileged Gitea action correlated with client-supplied X-WEBAUTH-USER (CVE-2026-20896) AIActions on Objectives High
Correlates privileged Gitea audit events (user deletion, access-token creation, admin actions, collaborator/permission changes, password resets) against reverse-proxy access logs showing an X-WEBAUTH-USER header for the same actor from an untrusted (non-loopback) source IP within a short window. This turns the CVE-2026-20896 header-spoof primitive into a confirmed privilege-escalation / account-impersonation event rather than a probe. Rationale: Ties the article's impact statement ('any source IP impersonate any user', admin accounts the obvious target) to concrete Gitea audit action names (token creation, user/repo deletion, collaborator changes) joined to the X-WEBAUTH-USER header value and a non-loopback client IP — a high-fidelity confirmation of exploitation, not just a scan. Corroborated by TheHackerNews reporting active exploitation. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-f75j-4cw6-rmx4 • https://thehackernews.com/2026/07/threat-actors-probe-gitea-docker-flaw.html • https://securityaffairs.com/194902/hacking/critical-gitea-docker-bug-under-active-exploitation-exposes-repositories-and-secrets.html
ATT&CKT1078.001 T1556
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate reverse-proxy SSO where the header genuinely originates from the trusted proxy appliance IP (excluded by the non-loopback filter only if the proxy is not on loopback — tune to your proxy CIDR)
  • Admin performing bulk maintenance via the intended proxy-authenticated path
  • Automated tenant provisioning scripts creating tokens for many users
  • Log parsing drift where the Gitea 'doer' field format differs by version, mis-attributing the actor
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Verify the correlated source IP is not the trusted proxy and that the actor account is privileged; pull the full Gitea audit trail for that actor in the window. Step 2 (contain): Revoke any tokens/SSH keys created during the window, force password reset and session invalidation for the impersonated account, restrict trusted-proxies to loopback/proxy CIDR, and patch to >=1.26.3. Step 3 (hunt): Enumerate all repos cloned, secrets/webhook URLs viewed, and CI pipelines triggered by the actor; check for outbound exfil from the Gitea host to the attacker source IP.
let win = 5m;
let proxyhdr = Syslog
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(24h)
    | where ProcessName in~ ("nginx","apache2","httpd")
    | where SyslogMessage has "x-webauth-user"
    | extend webauth_user = extract("(?i)x-webauth-user:? *([^ ]+)", 1, SyslogMessage)
    | extend clientip = extract("([0-9]+\\.[0-9]+\\.[0-9]+\\.[0-9]+)", 1, SyslogMessage)
    | where isnotempty(webauth_user) and clientip !startswith "127." and clientip != "::1"
    | project proxytime=TimeGenerated, webauth_user, clientip;
Syslog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(24h)
| where ProcessName in~ ("gitea","gitea-web")
| where SyslogMessage has_any ("delete_user","create_token","delete_repo","add_collaborator","reset_password","admin")
| extend actor = extract("(?i)(?:doer|user)[=: ]+([^ ,]+)", 1, SyslogMessage)
| where isnotempty(actor)
| join kind=inner proxyhdr on $left.actor == $right.webauth_user
| where proxytime between (TimeGenerated - win .. TimeGenerated + win)
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, actor, clientip, proxytime, SyslogMessage
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
index=web sourcetype=gitea (delete_user OR create_token OR delete_repo OR add_collaborator OR reset_password)
| rex field=_raw "(?i)(?:doer|user)[=: ]+(?<actor>[^ ,]+)"
| join type=inner max=0 actor
    [ search index=web (sourcetype=nginx:plus:access OR sourcetype=apache:access) "x-webauth-user"
      | rex field=_raw "(?i)x-webauth-user:? *(?<actor>[^ ]+)"
      | rex field=_raw "(?<client_ip>[0-9]+\.[0-9]+\.[0-9]+\.[0-9]+)"
      | where NOT cidrmatch("127.0.0.0/8", client_ip) ]
| stats count min(_time) AS firstTime max(_time) AS lastTime values(client_ip) AS source_ips by actor, host
| sort - count
Gitea container started with reverse-proxy auth exposing permissive trusted-proxies (CVE-2026-20896) AIInstallation Medium
Hunts the vulnerable deployment state itself: launch of a gitea/gitea Docker container with ENABLE_REVERSE_PROXY_AUTHENTICATION=true, which under the shipped image default REVERSE_PROXY_TRUSTED_PROXIES=* trusts X-WEBAUTH-USER from any source IP. Because the wildcard is baked into the image template (not the loopback-only default of app.example.ini), enabling reverse-proxy auth on any unpatched image (<=1.26.2) is sufficient to be exposed. Rationale: Uses the article's exact image name (gitea/gitea, verified 1.26.2) and the reproduction environment variable GITEA__service__ENABLE_REVERSE_PROXY_AUTHENTICATION=true — the combination that, with the baked-in REVERSE_PROXY_TRUSTED_PROXIES=* image default, produces the vulnerable state. Fixed version 1.26.3 confirmed via BleepingComputer and the GitLab advisory mirror. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-f75j-4cw6-rmx4 • https://advisories.gitlab.com/golang/code.gitea.io/gitea/CVE-2026-20896/ • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/hackers-exploit-critical-auth-bypass-in-gitea-docker-image/
ATT&CKT1556 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Deployment already patched to >=1.26.3 where the image template no longer hard-codes the wildcard
  • Admin who has explicitly set GITEA__security__REVERSE_PROXY_TRUSTED_PROXIES to a correct CIDR alongside enabling auth (safe config)
  • CI/CD test harness spinning up throwaway gitea/gitea containers that are never internet-exposed
  • IaC dry-run/plan output echoing the env var without actually launching a container
Suppression filter
| where ActingProcessCommandLine !has "gitea/gitea:1.26.3" and ActingProcessCommandLine !has "REVERSE_PROXY_TRUSTED_PROXIES=127.0.0.0/8"
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Confirm the running image tag is <=1.26.2 and inspect the effective app.ini for REVERSE_PROXY_TRUSTED_PROXIES (exec into container: cat /data/gitea/conf/app.ini). If it is '*' or absent, the instance is exposed. Step 2 (contain): Set REVERSE_PROXY_TRUSTED_PROXIES=127.0.0.0/8,::1/128 (or the real proxy CIDR), pull gitea/gitea:1.26.3+, redeploy, and ensure port 3000 is not directly internet-reachable. Step 3 (hunt): Review reverse-proxy and Gitea audit logs since container start for X-WEBAUTH-USER usage from untrusted IPs and any privileged actions on admin accounts.

Gitea webhook/migration SSRF reaching cloud metadata endpoints (IMDS / Azure WireServer) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Detects the Gitea server process making outbound connections to cloud instance-metadata endpoints (AWS/GCP IMDS 169.254.169.254, Azure WireServer 168.63.129.16, or the NAT64-embedded IMDS 64:ff9b::a9fe:a9fe) as a result of the CVE-2026-22874 SSRF allow-list bypass. A Gitea web application never legitimately queries instance metadata, so any such egress from the gitea binary is high-fidelity exploitation of the webhook/migration SSRF. Rationale: Uses the exact bypass IPs the advisory calls out (Azure WireServer 168.63.129.16, IMDS 169.254.169.254, NAT64 64:ff9b::a9fe:a9fe) scoped to the gitea process; a source-code host reaching IMDS is unambiguous SSRF exploitation. Cross-checked the go-gitea security advisory and Hive Security / cybersecurity-help write-ups confirming the non-blind SSRF and metadata reachability. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/go-gitea/gitea/security/advisories/GHSA-2r5c-gw76-rh3w • https://www.cybersecurity-help.cz/vdb/SB2026062240 • https://blog.gitea.com/release-of-1.26.3-and-1.26.4/
ATT&CKT1552.005 T1190
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security team validating the CVE-2026-22874 fix by intentionally pointing a webhook at 169.254.169.254
  • Authenticated vulnerability scanner running under the gitea service account probing metadata endpoints
  • A cloud-native monitoring/agent binary that happens to contain 'gitea' in its path also querying IMDS on the same host
  • Internal chaos/red-team exercise deliberately exercising the SSRF path
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the Gitea webhook history UI and repo migration logs for the affected instance around the connection time; identify which authenticated user created/edited the webhook or triggered the migration and the target URL. 2) Contain: disable the offending webhook/migration, force-rotate any cloud instance-role credentials or Azure managed-identity tokens the metadata endpoint could have leaked, and confirm Gitea is on 1.26.4+. 3) Hunt: review CloudTrail/Azure sign-in activity for use of the potentially stolen role credentials from unexpected IPs, and search for other Gitea egress to the SSRF bypass ranges (100.64.0.0/10, 172.32.0.0/11).

Gitea process egress to SSRF allow-list bypass internal ranges (CGNAT / 172.32.0.0/11) AIReconnaissance Medium
Hunts the Gitea server process connecting to internal ranges that Go's net.IP.IsPrivate() fails to block — RFC 6598 CGNAT (100.64.0.0/10) and the non-RFC1918 172.32.0.0/11 block — the ranges CVE-2026-22874 lets an authenticated user reach via webhook or migration. Surfaces internal-service probing/exfiltration that isn't a cloud-metadata hit. Rationale: Targets the exact non-RFC1918 ranges the advisory table lists as unblocked (RFC 6598 CGNAT 100.64.0.0/10 and 172.32.0.0/11) scoped to the gitea process — the internal-probing half of the SSRF that a metadata-IP rule would miss. Medium confidence because these ranges have legitimate uses (e.g. Tailscale/CGNAT overlays). Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/go-gitea/gitea/security/advisories/GHSA-2r5c-gw76-rh3w • https://www.cybersecurity-help.cz/vdb/SB2026062240
ATT&CKT1046 T1190
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Organization runs Tailscale or another overlay that legitimately assigns 100.64.0.0/10 addresses to internal services Gitea reaches
  • Legitimate self-hosted webhook receiver or CI runner living in a 172.32.0.0/11 internal subnet
  • Repository migration from a genuinely internal Git host in these ranges
  • CGNAT-addressed reverse proxy sitting in front of an approved webhook target
Suppression filter
| where not (RemoteIP startswith "100.100.")  // exclude Tailscale MagicDNS/coordination if in use; tune to your overlay's assigned block
Response runbook
1) Triage: correlate the destination IP with the Gitea webhook config / migration target and identify the authenticated user who set it; confirm whether the destination is a sanctioned internal service. 2) Contain: if unrecognized, disable the webhook/migration, and if the response body in webhook history contains sensitive internal data, treat the accessed service as exposed and rotate any secrets it returned. 3) Hunt: enumerate all webhooks and recent migrations across repos for targets in these ranges, and check whether the same user also hit metadata endpoints (UC1).
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName has "gitea"
| where ipv4_is_in_any_range(RemoteIP, "100.64.0.0/10", "172.32.0.0/11")
| summarize FirstSeen=min(Timestamp), LastSeen=max(Timestamp), Connections=count(), Ports=make_set(RemotePort,20) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, RemoteIP
| order by LastSeen desc
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ipv4_is_in_any_range(DestinationIP, "100.64.0.0/10", "172.32.0.0/11")
| where ApplicationProtocol has_any ("http","https") or DestinationPort in (80, 443, 8080, 8443)
| summarize FirstSeen=min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen=max(TimeGenerated), Connections=count(), DestPorts=make_set(DestinationPort,20) by SourceHostName, SourceIP, DestinationIP
| order by LastSeen desc
title: Gitea SSRF Egress to Non-RFC1918 Internal Ranges (CVE-2026-22874)

Vulnerable Gitea version exposed to CVE-2026-22874 SSRF (pre-1.26.4) AIWeaponization Medium
Inventory hunt for Gitea installations older than the patched 1.26.4 release that remain exposed to the incomplete SSRF allow-list in webhook delivery and repository migration. Prioritizes patching of internet-reachable Gitea instances before the bypass ranges can be abused. Rationale: Uses the concrete patched version (1.26.4) confirmed by the Gitea 1.26.3/1.26.4 release blog and the CVE id CVE-2026-22874 so the query flags every unpatched instance without a placeholder. Low FP because it is a deterministic version comparison against software inventory. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.gitea.com/release-of-1.26.3-and-1.26.4/ • https://github.com/go-gitea/gitea/security/advisories/GHSA-2r5c-gw76-rh3w
ATT&CKT1190
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A non-standard build/version string (e.g. '1.26.4+dev' or a distro backport) that trips the numeric parse but is actually patched
  • Forgejo or another Gitea fork carrying its own versioning that the SoftwareName 'gitea' match catches
  • Air-gapped or non-internet-facing Gitea where the SSRF risk is materially lower
  • Inventory lag showing an old version after an already-completed upgrade
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the reported SoftwareVersion against the live instance and whether it is internet-reachable or accepts webhooks/migrations from low-privilege users. 2) Contain: schedule/execute upgrade to Gitea 1.26.4+; as interim mitigation, restrict webhook and migration creation to trusted users and tighten the ALLOWED_HOST_LIST allow-list. 3) Hunt: for any instance found vulnerable, run UC1 and UC2 retroactively over the exposure window to determine whether the SSRF was already exercised against metadata or internal ranges.

| where Major < 1 or (Major == 1 and Minor < 26) or (Major == 1 and Minor == 26 and Patch < 4)
| project DeviceName, SoftwareVendor, SoftwareName, SoftwareVersion, EndOfSupportStatus
| order by DeviceName asc

Gitea same-user auth failure + success from different IPs within 2 minutes AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the CVE-2026-56750 remediation moment: when the victim logs in, Gitea detects the hash mismatch on the stolen token (a failed remember-me auth) while the attacker's real login succeeds from another IP. Correlates a Gitea auth failure and a Gitea auth success for the SAME user from DIFFERENT source IPs inside a 2-minute window. Rationale: Encodes the unique CVE signature described in the article: the hash-mismatch failure (victim's stolen token) and a concurrent success from a different IP (the live attacker/victim split). Same-user, mixed-result, multi-IP within 2 minutes is far more specific than either signal alone. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/golang/code.gitea.io/gitea/CVE-2026-56750/ • https://blog.gitea.com/release-of-1.27.0/
ATT&CKT1550.004 T1078
Data sourcesAuthentication.Authentication
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • User mistypes password (failure) then succeeds from a phone hotspot (different IP) within 2 minutes
  • Password-manager autofill fails on one device while the user manually logs in on another
  • MFA/2FA retry across two devices producing a failure then a success
  • Reverse proxy not preserving client IP so success and failure carry different source values
  • Scripted CI/CD login retry from a pool of runners with distinct egress IPs
Suppression filter
Restrict the failure side to true remember-me mismatches to cut ordinary typos: add `| where Results has "Failure"` context by pre-filtering ImAuthentication with `| where EventResult == "Success" or (EventResult == "Failure" and (EventResultDetails has "token" or EventResultDetails has "hash"))` before the summarize.
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: For the flagged user+window, confirm the failure is a Remember-Me/hash-mismatch (Gitea log line referencing ErrAuthTokenInvalidHash / auth_token) rather than a plain bad password, and compare the two source IPs' ASN/geo. 2) CONTAIN: Revoke the user's active sessions and remember-me tokens in Gitea, reset the password, and verify the instance is patched to >= 1.27.0 (PR #38406). 3) HUNT: Follow the successful IP forward for repo access, SSH-key or app-token creation, and pivot to UC #1/#2 to see if that token ID is still active from the attacker IP after the victim's login.
let Window = 2m;
ImAuthentication
| where TimeGenerated > ago(24h)
| where TargetAppName has "gitea" or EventProduct has "Gitea"
| where EventResult in ("Success", "Failure")
| summarize ResultVariety = dcount(EventResult), SrcCount = dcount(SrcIpAddr),
            Results = make_set(EventResult), Srcs = make_set(SrcIpAddr, 20)
        by TargetUsername, bin(TimeGenerated, Window)
| where ResultVariety > 1 and SrcCount > 1   // same user: both a failed and a successful Gitea auth from >1 IP in the same 2m bucket
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Vitest Browser Mode node.exe writes/deletes PNG or trace archive outside project into system paths AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts the arbitrary file-write/delete primitive of the @vitest/browser provider-command flaw (GHSA-p63j-vcc4-9vmv): the takeScreenshot/screenshotMatcher/stopChunkTrace/deleteTracing commands act on client-supplied absolute or `../`-traversed paths without an allowWrite check or project-root confinement. Surfaces the Node.js test-runner process creating .png / .zip (Playwright trace) files, or deleting files, inside Windows system directories where vitest artifacts never legitimately land. Rationale: Anchored on the advisory's named write/delete commands (takeScreenshot, screenshotMatcher, stopChunkTrace, deleteTracing) whose artifacts are always .png or Playwright trace .zip; node.exe + vitest cmdline + a system-dir target path is behaviour legitimate vitest (which writes to project __screenshots__) never exhibits. Cross-checked GHSA-p63j-vcc4-9vmv advisory page and Vitest browser.api docs for artifact types. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-p63j-vcc4-9vmv • https://vitest.dev/config/browser/api
ATT&CKT1565.001 T1070.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A vitest project genuinely configured with an output/screenshot directory under C:\ProgramData (unusual but possible in CI images)
  • Node-based Electron/desktop tooling writing cache PNGs under ProgramData while a vitest process is also running on the host
  • CI runner cleanup deleting stale trace .zip files under a ProgramData workspace
  • Antivirus/EDR quarantine moving a node-written file into a system path
  • Custom vitest reporter intentionally exporting artifacts to a shared ProgramData folder
Suppression filter
| where not (FolderPath has_any (@"\ProgramData\Jenkins\", @"\ProgramData\GitLab-Runner\") and InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "--reporter")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full FolderPath+FileName and the InitiatingProcessCommandLine; confirm the target is outside the repo's configured screenshot/trace directory and note whether a `../` or absolute path is implied by the location. Check whether the vitest process had browser.api/test.api bound to a non-loopback host. 2) Contain: kill the node.exe/vitest process, and if the API was network-exposed, block the listening port at the host firewall and isolate if the write hit System32/config or Startup. 3) Hunt: pivot on the same aid/DeviceName for inbound connections to ports 63315/51204 (UC2), for node.exe reads of credential files (.env, id_rsa, SAM), and upgrade @vitest/browser to 4.1.10 / 3.2.7 / 5.0.0-beta.6.

Vitest Browser Mode / test API (node.exe) accepting inbound connections on 63315/51204 from non-loopback host AIExploitation Medium
Detects the network-exposure precondition that makes GHSA-p63j-vcc4-9vmv remotely exploitable: the advisory states the flaw 'matters most when the Browser Mode API is exposed to the network' (test.api.host set / host 0.0.0.0). Surfaces node.exe running vitest accepting inbound connections on the default browser API port (63315) or test API port (51204) from a remote (non-loopback) source, meaning the file-touching provider commands are reachable off-box. Rationale: Uses the confirmed Vitest default ports (browser.api 63315, test.api 51204 per Vitest docs) plus node.exe + inbound + non-loopback source to isolate exactly the 'exposed to the network' condition the advisory calls out as the exploitable configuration. Ports and host=0.0.0.0 exposure confirmed against vitest.dev/config/browser/api. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-p63j-vcc4-9vmv • https://vitest.dev/config/browser/api • https://vitest.dev/config/api
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer intentionally running vitest browser mode across a trusted LAN (e.g. testing from a phone/VM) with host set to 0.0.0.0
  • CI/CD orchestrator on another host connecting to a shared test runner on the default port
  • Reverse-proxy or dev-tunnel (ngrok/localtunnel) forwarding to the vitest API for legitimate remote debugging
  • Container-to-host bridge traffic where the source IP is the docker bridge rather than true loopback
  • Security scanner sweeping the port range and hitting an open vitest API
Suppression filter
| where RemoteIPType == "Public"
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the RemoteIP is outside the developer's own workstation/expected LAN and whether it is public; capture the LocalPort and the vitest InitiatingProcessCommandLine to see if host was bound to 0.0.0.0/true. 2) Contain: block ports 63315/51204 inbound at the host firewall, rebind vitest to loopback, and if the remote source is untrusted/public, isolate the host and rotate any secrets reachable from the repo workspace. 3) Hunt: on the same host look for the file-write/delete primitive (UC1) and node.exe reads of credential files in the window following the inbound connection; confirm @vitest/browser version and patch to 4.1.10 / 3.2.7 / 5.0.0-beta.6.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "node.exe"
| where ActionType == "InboundConnectionAccepted"
| where LocalPort in (63315, 51204) or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("--browser.api","--api","test.api","vitest")
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" or (RemoteIP !in ("127.0.0.1","::1") and RemoteIPType != "Loopback")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, LocalPort, RemoteIP, RemoteIPType, RemotePort, InitiatingProcessId
| order by Timestamp desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where NetworkDirection == "Inbound"
| where DstPortNumber in (63315, 51204)
| where SrcIpAddr !in ("127.0.0.1","::1")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, DstHostname, DstPortNumber, SrcIpAddr, SrcPortNumber, NetworkApplicationProtocol
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Vulnerable @sigstore/oci (<=0.7.0) installed into node_modules — incl. transitive deps (CVE-2026-59891)
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Build/attestation process egress to non-canonical OCI registry (@sigstore/oci cred leak, CVE-2026-59891) · [LLM] actions/attest push-to-registry invocation using @sigstore/oci — externally influenced destination (CVE-2026-59891) · [LLM] Build/attestation tooling reading ~/.docker/config.json (credential harvest surface, CVE-2026-59891)
Build/attestation process egress to non-canonical OCI registry (@sigstore/oci cred leak, CVE-2026-59891) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts node/npm attestation tooling (@sigstore/oci consumers such as actions/attest*) opening a TLS session to a registry host that is NOT one of the canonical registries but resembles a registry endpoint. This is the observable manifestation of the substring-match credential confusion where a Docker auth key (e.g. ghcr.io) gets sent to an attacker registry (e.g. cr.io). Rationale: Anchors on the article's canonical registry aliases (ghcr.io/docker.io/index.docker.io/registry-1.docker.io) and the @sigstore/oci/attest consumer processes; flags a registry-shaped destination that is not in the canonical allowlist — the exact shape of a leaked credential going to an attacker registry like cr.io. Sentinel left empty as ImNetworkSession/CommonSecurityLog lack initiating-process attribution to node/build tooling on Linux CI hosts. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/@sigstore/oci/CVE-2026-59891/ • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/@cyclonedx/cdxgen/GHSA-qhh4-458h-xwh2/
ATT&CKT1552.001 T1048
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate push to a self-hosted/private registry (harbor.internal, jfrog artifactory) not in the allowlist
  • cosign/skopeo mirroring artifacts to a partner registry
  • node fetching a dependency from a mirror whose hostname contains 'registry'
  • cloud-provider registry endpoints in regions/formats not covered by the allowlist
Suppression filter
| where RemoteUrl !endswith ".internal" and RemoteUrl !has "artifactory" and RemoteUrl !has "harbor"
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full RemoteUrl/RemoteIP and the InitiatingProcessCommandLine — confirm whether the destination is a legitimate org registry or an unknown/typosquat host (e.g. cr.io vs ghcr.io). Check whether the destination substring-matches a configured Docker auth key. 2) Contain: if the host is unrecognised, block the domain/IP at egress, revoke and rotate the registry token(s) present in that runner's ~/.docker/config.json, and pause the affected CI pipeline. 3) Hunt: search all runners for @sigstore/oci <=0.7.0 (UC2), for the same non-canonical destination from other build hosts, and review who controlled the subject-name/image reference input for the pipeline.

Vulnerable @sigstore/oci (<=0.7.0) installed into node_modules — incl. transitive deps (CVE-2026-59891) AIDelivery High
Detects installation/presence of the vulnerable @sigstore/oci package (all releases 0.1.0 through 0.7.0) by watching for writes of its package files under node_modules. Catches transitive pulls (nested under @actions/attest or SBOM/provenance tooling) that developers may not expect from package.json alone. Rationale: Uses the exact vulnerable package coordinate @sigstore/oci from the advisory; the node_modules path write fires for both direct and transitive installs (the reviewer's transitive-dependency concern), and the FolderPath/TargetFilePath shows the parent package chain so an analyst can confirm whether it arrived via @actions/attest. Version <=0.7.0 confirmed by reading the written package.json. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/@sigstore/oci/CVE-2026-59891/ • https://www.npmjs.com/package/@sigstore/oci
ATT&CKT1195.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Host already remediated to @sigstore/oci 0.7.1 (version in written package.json is patched — benign)
  • CI cache warm/restore rehydrating node_modules from a prior clean build
  • Security team intentionally staging the vulnerable version in a test/repro sandbox
  • Monorepo with multiple node_modules trees generating repeated writes for the same package
Response runbook
1) Triage: read the written package.json 'version' field — if >= 0.7.1 it is patched, close; if <= 0.7.0 it is vulnerable. Note the parent path to see if it is a direct or transitive (@actions/attest) dependency. 2) Contain: on affected build hosts, bump @sigstore/oci to >=0.7.1 (and the actions/attest* actions that bundle it), then re-lock (package-lock.json/yarn.lock). 3) Hunt: enumerate every runner/image with the vulnerable version, and for any that ran attestation with push-to-registry:true against an externally-influenced subject-name, rotate the registry tokens in that host's Docker config.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where FolderPath has "node_modules" and FolderPath has "@sigstore/oci"
| where FileName in~ ("package.json","index.js")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")

| where TargetFileName in~ ("package.json","index.js")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

actions/attest push-to-registry invocation using @sigstore/oci — externally influenced destination (CVE-2026-59891) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Surfaces node/npm executions of actions/attest* (or direct @sigstore/oci usage) with push-to-registry enabled — the exact operation that reads ~/.docker/config.json credentials and selects them by substring match. Gives the SOC an inventory of vulnerable-pattern runs to review for an untrusted subject-name/image reference. Rationale: Uses the article's exact consumer names (@actions/attest, attest-build-provenance, attest-sbom) plus the push-to-registry:true / subject-name inputs that trigger the credential read from ~/.docker/config.json — an inventory of every place the vulnerable substring-match code path executes with an attacker-influenceable destination. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-pf56-329r-95rw • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/@sigstore/oci/CVE-2026-59891/
ATT&CKT1552.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: high — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Every legitimate provenance/SBOM attestation push in CI — this pattern is normal for signed-build pipelines
  • Internal release pipeline pushing to the org's own trusted registry with a fixed subject-name
  • Post-remediation runs on @sigstore/oci >=0.7.1 (still matches the pattern but no longer vulnerable)
  • Reusable workflow that always sets push-to-registry:true regardless of destination
Suppression filter
| where ProcessCommandLine !has "0.7.1" and ProcessCommandLine !has "0.7.2"
Response runbook
1) Triage: extract the subject-name/image reference from the command line and determine whether an untrusted party (fork PR, external contributor, upstream tag) could influence it, and whether @sigstore/oci on the host is <=0.7.0. 2) Contain: for pipelines where the reference is externally influenced, disable push-to-registry until @sigstore/oci is upgraded to >=0.7.1 and rotate the registry tokens in the runner's Docker config. 3) Hunt: correlate with UC1 (did this run also connect to a non-canonical registry?) and UC4 (docker config read), and review CI logs for the resolved destination host of each push.

Build/attestation tooling reading ~/.docker/config.json (credential harvest surface, CVE-2026-59891) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Detects node/npm/cosign/skopeo/docker build tooling accessing ~/.docker/config.json — the file @sigstore/oci's getRegistryCredentials() parses and substring-matches. Pairs the credential-read event with the process context so an analyst can spot a config read immediately before an anomalous registry connection. Rationale: Directly targets the article's named credential source (~/.docker/config.json) and the getRegistryCredentials() read behavior, scoped to attestation/OCI tooling so it is not a generic file-access dashboard. Note: Defender Linux read-event coverage is inconsistent — Falcon FileOpenInfo and auditd are the reliable sources here. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-pf56-329r-95rw • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/@cyclonedx/cdxgen/GHSA-qhh4-458h-xwh2/
ATT&CKT1552.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Normal `docker login`/`docker push` reading its own config during routine CI
  • cosign/skopeo authenticating to a legitimate registry during signing/mirroring
  • kubelet or buildkit reading config.json for a benign image pull
  • Developer running a local build that reads their personal Docker config
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "docker" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("login","pull") and FolderPath endswith "/.docker/config.json")
Response runbook
1) Triage: correlate the config.json read timestamp against the same host's outbound registry connections (UC1) — a read immediately followed by a connection to a non-canonical/registry-shaped host is the high-fidelity signal. 2) Contain: if the following connection is to an unrecognised registry, rotate every credential stored in that Docker config and block the destination at egress. 3) Hunt: check whether the reading process is @sigstore/oci <=0.7.0 (UC2) and whether the pipeline's subject-name/image reference was externally influenced; expand to all runners sharing that token.

Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-59891")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-59891")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

A new extortion cocktail: office printers, small ransoms, and BitLocker

Securelist (Kaspersky)
2026-07-21 11 use cases 7 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

We cover two recent cases of BitLocker extortion using RDP, MSSQL, RMM tools, web shells, and printers. The story includes TTPs and recommendations.

ATT&CKT1021.001 T1053.005 T1059.001 T1190 T1204.002 T1219 T1486

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] MSSQL sqlservr.exe spawning OS shell via xp_cmdshell (XEntry Team) · PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → Scheduled task created with suspicious image / encoded args · RMM tool installed by non-IT user — remote-access utility for hands-on-keyboard
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Unsanctioned RMM trio deployment: Endpoint Central, Mesh Agent, Tactical RMM · Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Server-side BitLocker mass encryption enablement (XEntry Team extortion) · [LLM] BitLocker recovery key harvesting via manage-bde -protectors -get · Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change · LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) · Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement
MSSQL sqlservr.exe spawning OS shell via xp_cmdshell (XEntry Team) AIExploitation High
Hunts the XEntry Team's initial execution vector: a misconfigured internet-exposed Microsoft SQL Server 2019 running commands through the xp_cmdshell extended stored procedure, which manifests as sqlservr.exe directly spawning cmd.exe/powershell.exe child processes. This is the pivot the actor used to move from DBMS access to full OS-level command execution. Rationale: Anchored on the article's explicit statement that Microsoft SQL Server 2019 was misconfigured to allow OS command execution via xp_cmdshell; sqlservr.exe spawning a shell is a high-fidelity, rarely-legitimate parent/child pair. Cross-checked against Huntress MSSQL xp_cmdshell attack writeups. Cross-checked against: • https://www.huntress.com/blog/attacking-mssql-servers-pt-ii • https://securelist.com/new-extortion-scheme-printers-bitlocker/120718/
ATT&CKT1059.003 T1505.001 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • DBA-approved xp_cmdshell jobs running maintenance batch scripts
  • SQL Agent jobs shelling out to robocopy/backup utilities
  • Monitoring agents that query OS metrics via xp_cmdshell
  • Legit ETL packages invoking cmd for file moves
Suppression filter
| where not (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("\\backup\\","robocopy","xp_cmdshell 'bcp") and InitiatingProcessAccountName endswith "$")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full ProcessCommandLine of the child and the SQL instance account context; determine if xp_cmdshell is intentionally enabled and whether this instance is internet-exposed on 1433. 2) Contain: disable xp_cmdshell (sp_configure), block inbound 1433 from internet ranges at the firewall, and rotate the SQL service/SA credentials — check GitHub for leaked connection strings. 3) Hunt: review subsequent processes on the host for RMM installs (Endpoint Central, Mesh Agent, Tactical RMM), web shell writes to IIS/wwwroot, and any manage-bde activity.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "sqlservr.exe"
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","net.exe","net1.exe","whoami.exe","reg.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where tolower(ParentProcessName) endswith "sqlservr.exe"
| where NewProcessName has_any ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","net.exe","net1.exe","whoami.exe","reg.exe")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, SubjectUserName, ParentProcessName, NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

description: Detects Microsoft SQL Server (sqlservr.exe) spawning a command interpreter or LOLBin child process, indicative of xp_cmdshell OS command execution as used by the XEntry Team.
references:

author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/07/21

    ParentImage|endswith: '\sqlservr.exe'

Server-side BitLocker mass encryption enablement (XEntry Team extortion) AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts the XEntry Team / RDP-BitLocker actor's impact stage: enabling BitLocker on servers and data drives via manage-bde -on or Enable-BitLocker with a numerical recovery password, deployed through RMM/scheduled tasks/GPO to encrypt disks and hold recovery keys for ransom. Targets encryption being turned ON on hosts (especially servers) where it is not part of normal provisioning. Rationale: Directly maps the article's core impact: 'enabling the BitLocker service and individually encrypting the infrastructure's disks, generating a key for each encrypted system.' manage-bde -on and Enable-BitLocker -RecoveryPassword are the exact commands that turn on encryption and generate a per-host recovery key. Corroborated by BlackHills/NCC BitLocker-ransomware research and Splunk Security Content. Cross-checked against: • https://www.blackhillsinfosec.com/bitlocker-ransomware-using-bitlocker-for-nefarious-reasons/ • https://research.splunk.com/endpoint/d0e6ec70-6e40-41a2-8b93-8d9ff077a746/
ATT&CKT1486 T1490 T1657
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • IT provisioning newly-imaged laptops with BitLocker via MDT/SCCM
  • Compliance-driven bulk BitLocker rollout on endpoints
  • Intune/GPO-managed BitLocker enablement on corporate fleet
  • Admin re-enabling BitLocker after a hardware/firmware change
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessParentFileName in~ ("ccmexec.exe","intunemanagementextension.exe","tsmanager.exe") or InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("ccmexec.exe","intunemanagementextension.exe"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm whether the host is a server/data store (unusual BitLocker target) and whether the enablement originated from an RMM agent, scheduled task, or GPO rather than your MDM baseline; capture the recovery key ID from the cmdline. 2) Contain: isolate the host, suspend the scheduled task/GPO driving the encryption domain-wide, and escrow/retrieve the recovery password from AD (msFVE-RecoveryInformation) before the attacker removes protectors. 3) Hunt: correlate for simultaneous manage-bde runs across many hosts, GPO changes on the DC, and RMM installs in the preceding days; look for 'Hacked by XEntry Team' strings and print-spooler ransom jobs.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where (FileName =~ "manage-bde.exe" and ProcessCommandLine has "-on")
    or (FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe") and ProcessCommandLine has_any ("Enable-BitLocker","-RecoveryPassword","-RecoveryPasswordProtector"))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessParentFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where (NewProcessName endswith "manage-bde.exe" and CommandLine has "-on")
    or (NewProcessName has_any ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe") and CommandLine has_any ("Enable-BitLocker","-RecoveryPassword"))
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, SubjectUserName, ParentProcessName, NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: BitLocker Encryption Enabled via manage-bde or Enable-BitLocker

description: Detects BitLocker being switched ON via manage-bde -on or the Enable-BitLocker PowerShell cmdlet with a recovery password, an extortion technique used by the XEntry Team to encrypt server disks.

Unsanctioned RMM trio deployment: Endpoint Central, Mesh Agent, Tactical RMM AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts the XEntry Team's C2/persistence layer: stacking multiple legitimate RMM tools (ManageEngine Endpoint Central, Mesh Agent, Tactical RMM) on victim hosts as a resilient control channel. The behavioural signal is the co-occurrence of MULTIPLE distinct RMM agent families on the same host or estate, which is abnormal for sanctioned IT. Rationale: The article names all three RMM families (ManageEngine Endpoint Central, Mesh Agent, Tactical RMM) deployed together for persistence/C2. Requiring >=2 distinct families on one host suppresses the noise of a single sanctioned RMM while catching the attacker's tool-stacking pattern. Kaspersky's report notes >20% of incidents abuse RMM for C2. Cross-checked against: • https://securelist.com/new-extortion-scheme-printers-bitlocker/120718/
ATT&CKT1219 T1105
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • MSP migration period where old and new RMM agents co-exist
  • IT trialing a replacement RMM before decommissioning the incumbent
  • Vendor support session installing a temporary Mesh/Tactical agent
  • Endpoint Central deployed alongside a separate patch-management RMM by design
Response runbook
1) Triage: verify each RMM family against your sanctioned-tooling inventory; identify the installer's parent process and the account/host that pushed it (RMM installed by sqlservr.exe or a scheduled task is highly suspect). 2) Contain: block the RMM cloud endpoints at the proxy, kill/uninstall the unauthorized agents, and disable the deploying scheduled task/GPO. 3) Hunt: pivot on the install account across the domain for lateral spread, check for BitLocker enablement and DC GPO modifications, and review outbound beaconing to Mesh/Tactical/Endpoint Central relay domains.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where FileName in~ ("meshagent.exe","tacticalrmm.exe","tacticalagent.exe","dcagentservice.exe","dcagenttrayicon.exe","uems.exe","desktopcentral.exe")
| extend Family = case(FileName has "mesh","MeshAgent", FileName has "tactical","TacticalRMM", "EndpointCentral")
| summarize Families = make_set(Family), Binaries = make_set(FileName), FirstSeen = min(Timestamp) by DeviceName
| where array_length(Families) >= 2
| order by FirstSeen desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where NewProcessName has_any ("meshagent.exe","tacticalrmm.exe","tacticalagent.exe","dcagentservice.exe","dcagenttrayicon.exe","UEMS.exe","DesktopCentral.exe")
| extend Family = case(NewProcessName has "mesh","MeshAgent", NewProcessName has "tactical","TacticalRMM", "EndpointCentral")
| summarize Families = make_set(Family), Binaries = make_set(NewProcessName), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated) by Computer
| where array_length(Families) >= 2
| order by FirstSeen desc
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 @Image:(*\meshagent.exe OR *\MeshAgent.exe OR *\tacticalrmm.exe OR *\tacticalagent.exe OR *\dcagentservice.exe OR *\dcagenttrayicon.exe OR *\UEMS.exe OR *\DesktopCentral.exe)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ImageFileName=/\\(meshagent|tacticalrmm|tacticalagent|dcagentservice|dcagenttrayicon|UEMS|DesktopCentral)\.exe$/i

| groupBy([aid, ComputerName], function=collect([family]))
| length(family) >= 2
ENDPOINT

BitLocker recovery key harvesting via manage-bde -protectors -get AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts the collection step where the XEntry Team used RMM tooling to enumerate and exfiltrate per-host BitLocker recovery keys before/after encryption, via manage-bde -protectors -get or (Get-BitLockerVolume).KeyProtector. Distinct pivot from the encryption-enable UC: this catches key extraction, which is how the actor retains the ransom leverage. Rationale: Maps the article's caption 'Commands executed through RMM tools to collect BitLocker keys.' manage-bde -protectors -get and Get-BitLockerVolume KeyProtector are the canonical ways to read the recovery password, and reading keys at scale (especially from an RMM parent) is rare in benign ops. Corroborated by BlackHills BitLocker-ransomware detection guidance. Cross-checked against: • https://www.blackhillsinfosec.com/bitlocker-ransomware-using-bitlocker-for-nefarious-reasons/ • https://securelist.com/new-extortion-scheme-printers-bitlocker/120718/
ATT&CKT1005 T1552.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Helpdesk retrieving a recovery key for a locked-out user
  • MDM/Intune escrow validation scripts reading KeyProtector
  • Sysadmin auditing BitLocker protector status during compliance checks
  • Backup software querying BitLocker volume state
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("intunemanagementextension.exe","ccmexec.exe") or AccountName endswith "-helpdesk")
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the parent process and account — key enumeration launched from an RMM agent, sqlservr.exe, or a scheduled task on multiple hosts is the attacker pattern, whereas a single helpdesk invocation is benign. 2) Contain: if unsanctioned, isolate affected hosts, force-rotate BitLocker keys and re-escrow to AD/Azure, and revoke the executing account. 3) Hunt: correlate with manage-bde -on enablement events, RMM installs, and any staging/exfil of the collected keys to attacker infrastructure; expand across all domain-synced systems.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where (FileName =~ "manage-bde.exe" and ProcessCommandLine has "-protectors" and ProcessCommandLine has "-get")
    or (FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe") and ProcessCommandLine has_any ("Get-BitLockerVolume","KeyProtector","RecoveryPassword"))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessParentFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where (NewProcessName endswith "manage-bde.exe" and CommandLine has "-protectors" and CommandLine has "-get")
    or (NewProcessName has_any ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe") and CommandLine has_any ("Get-BitLockerVolume","KeyProtector","RecoveryPassword"))
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, SubjectUserName, ParentProcessName, NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: BitLocker Recovery Key Enumeration via manage-bde or Get-BitLockerVolume
id: c7e41b8a-2f60-4d93-a1c5-9b7f3e2a6d18
description: Detects enumeration of BitLocker recovery key protectors via manage-bde -protectors -get or Get-BitLockerVolume KeyProtector, used by the XEntry Team to harvest per-host recovery keys for extortion.
references:
  - https://securelist.com/new-extortion-scheme-printers-bitlocker/120718/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/07/21
tags:
  - attack.t1005
  - attack.t1552.001
logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: windows
detection:
  managebde:
    Image|endswith: '\manage-bde.exe'
    CommandLine|contains|all:
      - '-protectors'
      - '-get'
  psbitlocker:
    Image|endswith:
      - '\powershell.exe'
      - '\pwsh.exe'
    CommandLine|contains:
      - 'Get-BitLockerVolume'
      - 'KeyProtector'
      - 'RecoveryPassword'
  condition: managebde or psbitlocker
level: medium
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 ((@Image:(*\manage-bde.exe OR *\MANAGE-BDE.EXE) AND @CommandLine:*-protectors* AND @CommandLine:*-get*) OR (@Image:(*\powershell.exe OR *\PowerShell.exe) AND @CommandLine:(*Get-BitLockerVolume* OR *KeyProtector* OR *RecoveryPassword*)))
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2
| (ImageFileName=/\\manage-bde\.exe$/i AND CommandLine=/-protectors/i AND CommandLine=/-get/i) OR (ImageFileName=/\\(powershell|pwsh)\.exe$/i AND CommandLine=/(Get-BitLockerVolume|KeyProtector|RecoveryPassword)/i)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, CommandLine], function=count())
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where (Processes.process_name="manage-bde.exe" AND Processes.process="*-protectors*" AND Processes.process="*-get*") OR (Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe") AND (Processes.process="*Get-BitLockerVolume*" OR Processes.process="*KeyProtector*" OR Processes.process="*RecoveryPassword*")) by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.parent_process_name Processes.process_name Processes.process | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where (Processes.process_name="manage-bde.exe" AND Processes.process="*-protectors*" AND Processes.process="*-get*") OR (Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe") AND (Processes.process="*Get-BitLockerVolume*" OR Processes.process="*KeyProtector*" OR Processes.process="*RecoveryPassword*")) by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.parent_process_name Processes.process_name Processes.process | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc
NETWORK
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) Actions on Objectives High
Mimikatz, comsvcs.dll MiniDump, or any non-Windows process opening LSASS.
ATT&CKT1003.001 T1003

DeviceEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)

| where ActionType == "OpenProcessApiCall"
| where FileName =~ "lsass.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","csrss.exe",
                                          "svchost.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe",
                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent EventID 4663 = object access. AccessMask 0x10 / 0x40 / 0x1010
// indicate read access to lsass.exe — credential-dump signature. Audit
// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4663
| where ObjectName endswith "\lsass.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ObjectName, ProcessName,
          AccessMask, AccessReason
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Remote-Management (RMM) Tool Execution
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d0c

Iran War Cyber Threat Landscape | A Midyear Assessment on What Matters

SentinelLabs
2026-07-21 8 use cases 2 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

In April, SentinelLABS’ Tom Hegel published an initial assessment of the first five weeks of the conflict. Three months later, the evidence supports refinement.
