Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Masqueraded uxtheme.dll dropped outside Windows dirs via SysAid/RMM deployment (Cavern Manticore) · Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] AppDomainManager hijacking config artifact dropped in user-writable path (Screening Serpens/UNC1549) · Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Cavern Manticore WinDirStat DLL side-load of fake uxtheme.dll (Cavern backdoor) · RMM tool installed by non-IT user — remote-access utility for hands-on-keyboard
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Cavern Manticore WinDirStat DLL side-load of fake uxtheme.dll (Cavern backdoor) AIInstallation High
Hunts the specific defense-evasion step where a legitimate signed WinDirStat binary loads an attacker-supplied uxtheme.dll (the Cavern modular C2 backdoor) from its own directory instead of System32. This is the exact side-load Check Point documented for the Iran-nexus Cavern Manticore actor deployed via abused SysAid/RMM access. Rationale: windirstat.exe + a co-located uxtheme.dll outside Windows system dirs is the exact side-load Check Point named for Cavern Manticore; uxtheme.dll almost never loads from a non-Windows path legitimately, making this near-zero FP. Cross-checked Check Point Research and cybersecuritynews write-ups. Cross-checked against: • https://research.checkpoint.com/2026/cavern-manticore-exposing-iran-linked-modular-c2-framework/ • https://cybersecuritynews.com/cavern-manticore-abuses-sysaid-rmm-and-windirstat-dll-sideloading/
ATT&CKT1574.002 T1036.005
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Portable/extracted WinDirStat build run from a Downloads or Temp folder that ships its own theming redistributable
  • Malware-analysis sandbox that stages system DLLs alongside sample binaries
  • Developer copying a Windows uxtheme.dll next to a test build for debugging
  • Application-virtualization (App-V/MSIX) layers presenting DLLs from a virtual path
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFolderPath !startswith @"C:\Program Files"
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the DllSHA256 and HostSHA256 from the hit; confirm windirstat.exe is the genuine signed binary and the uxtheme.dll is unsigned/unknown — a mismatch confirms side-load. 2) Contain: isolate the host, capture the folder containing both files, and block the DLL hash across the fleet. 3) Hunt: check how the bundle arrived — look for SysAid/RMM agent processes writing these files, and pivot on the host's outbound connections for Cavern modular C2 beacons.
DeviceImageLoadEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "windirstat.exe"
| where FileName =~ "uxtheme.dll"
| where FolderPath !startswith @"C:\Windows\System32" and FolderPath !startswith @"C:\Windows\SysWOW64" and FolderPath !startswith @"C:\Windows\WinSxS"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, HostProcess=InitiatingProcessFolderPath, HostSHA256=InitiatingProcessSHA256, LoadedDllPath=FolderPath, DllSHA256=SHA256, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
Event
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where Source == "Microsoft-Windows-Sysmon" and EventID == 7
| where EventData has "windirstat.exe" and EventData has "uxtheme.dll"
| where EventData !has @"C:\Windows\System32" and EventData !has @"C:\Windows\SysWOW64" and EventData !has @"C:\Windows\WinSxS"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, EventID, RenderedDescription

Masqueraded uxtheme.dll dropped outside Windows dirs via SysAid/RMM deployment (Cavern Manticore) AIDelivery High
Hunts the delivery/lateral-movement step where Cavern Manticore abuses an existing SysAid software-deployment feature (or other RMM agent) to write the WinDirStat bundle — a fake uxtheme.dll masquerading as a system DLL — onto a target machine. Surfaces the writing (parent) process so the abused RMM/deployment tool is identified without hardcoding its name. Rationale: uxtheme.dll is a core Windows DLL that legitimately exists only under C:\Windows; any write elsewhere is the Cavern Manticore masqueraded backdoor. Surfacing the WriterProcess/WriterParent identifies the abused SysAid/RMM deployment tool (article's key point: no SysAid CVE, legitimate feature abused). Cross-checked Check Point and cybersecuritynews. Cross-checked against: • https://research.checkpoint.com/2026/cavern-manticore-exposing-iran-linked-modular-c2-framework/ • https://cybersecuritynews.com/cavern-manticore-abuses-sysaid-rmm-and-windirstat-dll-sideloading/
ATT&CKT1072 T1574.002 T1036.005
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Portable WinDirStat distribution unzipped to a user folder that bundles a themed DLL
  • Software packaging/repackaging tools staging system DLLs into a build directory under ProgramData
  • Backup or migration tooling restoring a full profile tree that includes a copied uxtheme.dll
  • Endpoint DLL-redirection/app-compat shim tooling copying system DLLs beside an app
Suppression filter
| where WriterProcess !in~ ("msiexec.exe","7z.exe","winrar.exe","explorer.exe") or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("sysaid","rmm","deploy")
Response runbook
1) Triage: hash the dropped uxtheme.dll and inspect WriterProcess/WriterParent — if the writer is a SysAid/RMM agent or an unexpected service account, treat as active deployment-tool abuse. 2) Contain: isolate the destination host, suspend the RMM deployment job/account, and revoke the RMM agent's credentials pending review. 3) Hunt: enumerate every host that received the same job from that RMM console, and hunt for windirstat.exe loading the DLL plus Cavern modular C2 egress.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName =~ "uxtheme.dll"
| where FolderPath !startswith @"C:\Windows\"
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, DroppedDllPath=FolderPath, SHA256, WriterProcess=InitiatingProcessFileName, WriterPath=InitiatingProcessFolderPath, WriterCmd=InitiatingProcessCommandLine, WriterParent=InitiatingProcessParentFileName
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventType == "FileCreated"
| where TargetFileName =~ "uxtheme.dll"
| where TargetFilePath !startswith @"C:\Windows\"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetFilePath, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine

AppDomainManager hijacking config artifact dropped in user-writable path (Screening Serpens/UNC1549) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the .NET AppDomainManager hijack Unit 42 flagged as Screening Serpens' key 2026 evolution: an application .exe.config is planted in a user-writable directory (Downloads/Temp/AppData/ProgramData/Public) to force a signed .NET host to load a malicious managed assembly (MiniUpdate/MiniJunk V2) before EDR inspects it. Config drops of this kind in those paths are rare outside developer tooling. Rationale: AppDomainManager hijacking (the technique Unit 42 named as Screening Serpens' critical 2026 evolution) requires a planted .exe.config redirecting a signed .NET host to a malicious assembly; a .exe.config appearing in Downloads/Temp/AppData outside dev tooling is the detectable artifact. Content isn't in file telemetry, so this is scoped as hunting. Cross-checked Unit 42 and cybersecuritynews. Cross-checked against: • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/tracking-iran-apt-screening-serpens/ • https://cybersecuritynews.com/iranian-hackers-abuse-appdomainmanager-hijacking/
ATT&CKT1574.014 T1036.005
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer building/running .NET apps from a Downloads or repo folder under AppData
  • ClickOnce or portable .NET apps that ship their own app.config beside the exe in ProgramData
  • CI/build agents or unit-test runners emitting *.exe.config into temp build directories
  • Legacy line-of-business tools installed to a per-user AppData location with a bundled config
Suppression filter
| where WriterProcess !in~ ("dotnet.exe","devenv.exe","msbuild.exe","testhost.exe","vstest.console.exe") and WriterPath !contains @"\Visual Studio"
Response runbook
1) Triage: open the flagged .exe.config and check for appDomainManagerAssembly/appDomainManagerType (or a legacyCorruptedState/startup redirect) plus a sibling unsigned DLL — those confirm AppDomainManager hijacking; also note the sibling .exe's signer. 2) Contain: isolate the host, block the sibling assembly hash, and preserve the folder for IR. 3) Hunt: pivot to the .NET host process executing from that folder, look for MiniUpdate/MiniJunk V2 C2 (including Azure-fronted beacons), and search other hosts for the same recruitment-lure delivery path.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName endswith ".exe.config"
| where FolderPath has_any (@"\Downloads\", @"\Temp\", @"\AppData\", @"\ProgramData\", @"\Users\Public\")
| where FolderPath !contains @"\Microsoft\" and FolderPath !contains @"\Programs\"
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("msiexec.exe","devenv.exe","msbuild.exe","dotnet.exe","setup.exe","officeclicktorun.exe","vs_installer.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, ConfigPath=FolderPath, ConfigFile=FileName, WriterProcess=InitiatingProcessFileName, WriterPath=InitiatingProcessFolderPath, WriterCmd=InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventType == "FileCreated"
| where TargetFileName endswith ".exe.config"
| where TargetFilePath has_any (@"\Downloads\", @"\Temp\", @"\AppData\", @"\ProgramData\", @"\Users\Public\")
| where TargetFilePath !contains @"\Microsoft\" and TargetFilePath !contains @"\Programs\"
| where ActingProcessName !in~ ("msiexec.exe","devenv.exe","msbuild.exe","dotnet.exe","setup.exe")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

New Project CAV3RN module abuses Outlook calendar events for C2 and DNS AAAA records for configuration recovery

Securelist (Kaspersky)
2026-07-21 9 use cases 2 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

Kaspersky GReAT experts describe a new Project CAV3RN C2 module. It uses Outlook calendar for communication via Microsoft Graph and has a backup connection via DNS AAAA responses.

Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).

Installation. Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] CAV3RN/HOLLOWGRAPH DNS AAAA config-recovery beaconing to cloudlanecdn[.]com · [LLM] Anomalous DLL-host process reaching Microsoft Graph/login.microsoftonline for calendar C2 · Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → OAuth consent / suspicious app grant
CAV3RN/HOLLOWGRAPH DNS AAAA config-recovery beaconing to cloudlanecdn[.]com AICommand & Control High
Hunts the DNS-based fallback channel AzureCommunication.dll uses when Microsoft Graph auth fails: AAAA queries to actor-controlled cloudlanecdn.com and its subdomains, which encode configuration fragments (tenant ID, client ID/secret, mailbox) inside IPv6 answers. The .p. (probe length) and .q. (retrieve) label structure and long hex data labels are unique to this module. Rationale: Anchored on the confirmed C2 domain cloudlanecdn.com plus the AAAA query type and the .p./.q. label structure documented in the article (example d.53466d4c67515a.0.p.cloudlanecdn.com); cross-checked against Group-IB and Check Point who both cite cloudlanecdn.com as actor infrastructure. Cross-checked against: • https://www.group-ib.com/blog/hollowgraph-microsoft-365/ • https://research.checkpoint.com/2026/cavern-manticore-exposing-iran-linked-modular-c2-framework/ • https://thehackernews.com/2026/07/hollowgraph-malware-hides-c2-and-stolen.html
ATT&CKT1071.004 T1132.001 T1008
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution.DNS
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimately registered internal service that happens to share the cloudlanecdn.com string (none known — domain is exclusively malicious)
  • Security scanners / DNS sinkholes actively resolving the IOC domain for research
  • Threat-intel enrichment tooling pre-resolving IOC feeds
  • Passive DNS mirroring appliances replaying the query
Suppression filter
| where not(Computer has_any ("sinkhole","ti-enrich","dns-research"))
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Identify the querying host and the process that initiated the AAAA lookup; pull any preceding failed Graph/login.microsoftonline.com auth on the same host. Step 2 (contain): Isolate the host, sinkhole/block *.cloudlanecdn.com at DNS and egress, and treat the associated M365 mailbox/app registration as compromised — revoke the service-principal client secret. Step 3 (hunt): Sweep all DNS logs for other hosts querying cloudlanecdn.com or its ns1-4 nameservers, and look for logAzure.txt drops and AzureCommunication.dll/uxtheme.dll on those hosts.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteUrl endswith "cloudlanecdn.com"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| order by Timestamp desc
// Defender does not expose DNS query-type; any resolution/connection to cloudlanecdn.com is the C2 domain
DnsEvents
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where QueryTypeName == "AAAA"
| where Name endswith "cloudlanecdn.com"
| extend ConfigRecovery = iff(Name matches regex @"\.(p|q)\.cloudlanecdn\.com$", "yes", "no")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, ClientIP, Name, QueryTypeName, ResultCodeName, ConfigRecovery
| order by TimeGenerated desc

CAV3RN AzureCommunication.dll config file 'logAzure.txt' written to disk AIInstallation High
Hunts creation of the module's on-disk configuration cache logAzure.txt, which AzureCommunication.dll writes (serialized JSON containing TenantId, ClientId, ClientSecret, target mailbox, DNS bootstrap host and RSA keys) when no prior config file exists. The filename is hardcoded and resolved against the host process working directory, making it a high-fidelity single-string artifact. Rationale: logAzure.txt is a hardcoded, unique filename from the article that stores the module's live Graph credentials and RSA keys — its creation is a near-zero-FP install/staging artifact. Cross-checked against: • https://www.group-ib.com/blog/hollowgraph-microsoft-365/ • https://securelist.com/project-cav3rn-cyberespionage-framework-using-outlook-and-dns/120757/
ATT&CKT1074.001 T1552.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • An unrelated bespoke application coincidentally naming an Azure log file logAzure.txt (rare — most Azure SDK logs use different names)
  • Developer/test tooling on an engineer workstation writing a similarly named debug log
  • Backup/restore operations replaying a captured filesystem containing the artifact
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFolderPath !has "\\Program Files\\Microsoft"
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Read the folder path and the initiating process — the working directory reveals the host process loading the DLL; capture that process and any sibling DLLs. Step 2 (contain): Isolate the host and preserve logAzure.txt for forensics (it exposes the compromised tenant/mailbox and app client secret), then revoke the exposed service-principal secret and rotate the mailbox credentials. Step 3 (hunt): Pivot on the initiating process hash across the fleet, hunt for AzureCommunication.dll/uxtheme.dll/n-HTCommp.dll and cloudlanecdn.com DNS.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType == "FileCreated"
| where FileName =~ "logAzure.txt"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FolderPath, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventType == "FileCreated"
| where TargetFileName =~ "logAzure.txt"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, TargetFilePath, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, ActorUsername
| order by TimeGenerated desc

CAV3RN framework module DLLs loaded/dropped (AzureCommunication / n-HTCommp / masqueraded uxtheme) AIInstallation Medium
Hunts the CAV3RN Native-AOT components by distinctive filename, known MD5, and by uxtheme.dll masquerading (a legitimate System32 DLL name loaded from a non-system path). Catches both the current AzureCommunication.dll communication module and the prior n-HTCommp.dll, plus the uxtheme.dll controller. Rationale: Uses the exact module filenames (AzureCommunication.dll, n-HTCommp.dll) and the three article MD5s, plus the uxtheme.dll masquerade heuristic (legit name off the system path) — all directly named in the article and corroborated by Check Point/Group-IB as CAV3RN/Cavern components. Cross-checked against: • https://research.checkpoint.com/2026/cavern-manticore-exposing-iran-linked-modular-c2-framework/ • https://www.group-ib.com/blog/hollowgraph-microsoft-365/
ATT&CKT1036.005 T1574.001 T1027.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A third-party product legitimately shipping a file named AzureCommunication.dll (uncommon — no known benign product uses this name)
  • uxtheme.dll present in a legitimate application-local WinSxS-style redirection folder not covered by the path exclusions
  • Software installers temporarily extracting a uxtheme.dll to a temp path during patching
  • Antivirus quarantine/restore replaying the sample hash
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFolderPath !startswith "C:\\Program Files"
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Confirm the load path and initiating (host) process; a non-system uxtheme.dll or an AzureCommunication.dll under a user-writable path is high confidence. Step 2 (contain): Isolate the host, block the MD5s at EDR, and quarantine the DLLs; treat the tenant/mailbox in any co-resident logAzure.txt as compromised. Step 3 (hunt): Pivot the host process and hashes fleet-wide, then correlate with cloudlanecdn.com DNS and graph.microsoft.com egress from the same hosts.

Anomalous DLL-host process reaching Microsoft Graph/login.microsoftonline for calendar C2 AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts the Outlook-calendar dead-drop C2: a DLL-host process (rundll32/regsvr32/dllhost) — not a browser or Office app — authenticating to login.microsoftonline.com and calling graph.microsoft.com, which is how AzureCommunication.dll acquires an app token and reads/writes calendar events. Pairs the unusual process with legitimate-but-abused Microsoft cloud endpoints. Rationale: Ties the article's Graph OAuth client_credentials flow (login.microsoftonline.com/.../oauth2/v2.0/token) and graph.microsoft.com organization/calendar calls to an atypical DLL-host process, since AzureCommunication.dll is a Native-AOT DLL executed via a controller rather than a browser/Office client; kept hunting-tier because the cloud endpoints are legitimately used. Cross-checked against: • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/new-hollowgraph-malware-uses-microsoft-graph-for-stealthy-c2-comms/ • https://www.group-ib.com/blog/hollowgraph-microsoft-365/
ATT&CKT1102.002 T1071.001 T1550.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • rundll32/dllhost hosting a legitimate Office/OneDrive/Teams COM component that brokers a Graph call
  • Enterprise device-management or licensing agents invoking Graph via a scripted rundll32 wrapper
  • IT automation scripts using regsvr32/rundll32 shims that touch login.microsoftonline.com for token acquisition
  • A signed line-of-business add-in running from AppData that legitimately uses Microsoft Graph
Suppression filter
| where not(InitiatingProcessFolderPath startswith "C:\\Program Files" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("OneDrive","Teams","Office"))
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Inspect the initiating process command line and loaded modules — look for a Native-AOT DLL exporting QueryInterface or a co-resident logAzure.txt / cloudlanecdn.com resolution on the same host. Step 2 (contain): If confirmed, isolate the host, revoke the app registration's client secret and the mailbox tokens, and block egress for that process. Step 3 (hunt): Enumerate calendar events dated 2050 (subject prefix 'Event ID:' / suffix '1500') in the implicated mailbox via Graph/eDiscovery, and sweep other hosts for the same DLL-host→Graph pattern.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where RemoteUrl has_any ("graph.microsoft.com","login.microsoftonline.com")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","dllhost.exe")
    or InitiatingProcessFolderPath has_any ("\\AppData\\","\\Temp\\","\\ProgramData\\","\\Public\\")
| summarize FirstSeen=min(Timestamp), Endpoints=make_set(RemoteUrl), Count=count() by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| where array_length(Endpoints) >= 1
| order by FirstSeen desc
ASCDeviceEvents
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where RemoteUrl has_any ("graph.microsoft.com","login.microsoftonline.com")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","dllhost.exe")
| summarize FirstSeen=min(TimeGenerated), Endpoints=make_set(RemoteUrl), Count=count() by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by FirstSeen desc

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc
NETWORK
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("CAF021DDA726B8BA049C2AA395E505A1", "C092B02FBC0FDF7EE9608DD016673806", "29B2B8C5D99F05BFCDD0D8D976EB5678") or SHA1 in~ ("CAF021DDA726B8BA049C2AA395E505A1", "C092B02FBC0FDF7EE9608DD016673806", "29B2B8C5D99F05BFCDD0D8D976EB5678") or MD5 in~ ("CAF021DDA726B8BA049C2AA395E505A1", "C092B02FBC0FDF7EE9608DD016673806", "29B2B8C5D99F05BFCDD0D8D976EB5678")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("CAF021DDA726B8BA049C2AA395E505A1", "C092B02FBC0FDF7EE9608DD016673806", "29B2B8C5D99F05BFCDD0D8D976EB5678") or SHA1 in~ ("CAF021DDA726B8BA049C2AA395E505A1", "C092B02FBC0FDF7EE9608DD016673806", "29B2B8C5D99F05BFCDD0D8D976EB5678") or MD5 in~ ("CAF021DDA726B8BA049C2AA395E505A1", "C092B02FBC0FDF7EE9608DD016673806", "29B2B8C5D99F05BFCDD0D8D976EB5678")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["CAF021DDA726B8BA049C2AA395E505A1", "C092B02FBC0FDF7EE9608DD016673806", "29B2B8C5D99F05BFCDD0D8D976EB5678"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Vulnerable node-tar (<=7.5.18) present on hosts — CVE-2026-59873 decompression-bomb DoS exposure AIWeaponization High
Surfaces every host where Defender/vuln-scanner telemetry reports the npm 'tar' package at or below 7.5.18, the range vulnerable to CVE-2026-59873 (unbounded decompressed-data DoS / Gzip bomb). This is the attack-surface inventory pivot: any service extracting untrusted archives with this version can be forced to exhaust disk/CPU with a tiny crafted input. Fixed in 7.5.19. Rationale: Pivots on the exact CVE id and the concrete affected range from the advisory (npm 'tar' <= 7.5.18, fixed 7.5.19). CVE-id equality in TVM/scanner data is a high-fidelity exposure list with near-zero FP — the only 'noise' is remediated-but-not-rescanned hosts. Cross-checked against: • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2026-59874 • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-23hp-3jrh-7fpw • https://www.tenable.com/cve/CVE-2026-59874 • https://github.com/isaacs/node-tar/security
ATT&CKT1499.004
Data sourcesVulnerabilities.Vulnerabilities
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Host patched to 7.5.19+ but not yet re-scanned, so TVM snapshot is stale
  • 'tar' package pinned inside a container image layer that never extracts untrusted input (internal build tarballs only)
  • Nested/transitive copy of tar in node_modules of a dev-only dependency not reachable at runtime
  • Scanner fingerprints a vendored copy of tar sources without a functioning install
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the flagged host actually runs a service that extracts attacker-supplied archives (npm registry mirror, CI/CD artifact unpack, file-upload/import feature) — internal-only tarball handling is lower priority. 2) Contain: schedule `npm i tar@7.5.19` (or bump the transitive pin via overrides/resolutions) on internet-facing extractors first; add a temporary reverse-proxy body-size cap on upload endpoints. 3) Hunt: pull last 7d of file-write telemetry for the host's node processes and run UC2 to check whether a decompression bomb was already attempted.

node.js process rapidly writing multi-GB file during archive extraction (CVE-2026-59873 gzip-bomb impact) AIActions on Objectives Low
Hunts the runtime signature of the node-tar decompression bomb: a node process extracting an archive and writing an abnormally large single file (multi-GB) from a tiny compressed input, driving toward disk exhaustion. Article-specific because the vuln has no total-bytes/entry-count guard, so extraction writes far more data than the input warrants. Rationale: Targets the article's core impact — node-tar writes gigabytes to disk from a negligible compressed stream because there is no cumulative-bytes limit. Pivots on node as the initiating process plus an anomalous single-file size / write-volume, the observable footprint of the Gzip bomb described in the PoC. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-23hp-3jrh-7fpw • https://github.com/isaacs/node-tar/security
ATT&CKT1499.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: high — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate large-artifact extraction in CI (multi-GB container layers, ML model tarballs, dataset archives)
  • Node-based backup/restore or migration jobs that genuinely unpack large archives
  • Log-rotation or cache builds where a node service writes a large single file
  • Package-mirror sync (e.g. Verdaccio/nexus) unpacking large legitimate packages
  • Electron/build tooling emitting a large bundled asset
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("backup","restore","webpack","electron-builder","model","dataset") or FolderPath has_any ("\\ci\\","\\build\\","\\artifacts\\","/ci/","/build/","/artifacts/"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: check the InitiatingProcessCommandLine and FolderPath — is this a known CI/build/backup job writing to an expected artifact path, or an internet-facing upload/import service writing to a temp/extraction dir? Correlate the host against UC1 (is tar<=7.5.18 present?). 2) Contain: if unexplained, kill the node PID and quarantine the source archive; add disk-quota/ulimit on the extraction directory and a body-size limit on the upload endpoint. 3) Hunt: search the same host+window for the inbound tiny archive (small POST/upload immediately preceding the write burst) and for repeated attempts across other extractor hosts.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","node")
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")
| summarize MaxFileSize = max(FileSize), Writes = count(), FirstWrite = min(Timestamp), LastWrite = max(Timestamp) by DeviceName, FolderPath, FileName, InitiatingProcessId, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| where MaxFileSize > 2147483648   // >2GB single file from a node extraction; gzip-bomb hallmark vs tiny input
| extend WriteWindowSec = datetime_diff('second', LastWrite, FirstWrite)
| order by MaxFileSize desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActingProcessName has "node"
| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")
| summarize MaxFileSize = max(TargetFileSize), Writes = count(), FirstWrite = min(TimeGenerated), LastWrite = max(TimeGenerated) by DvcHostname, TargetFilePath, ActingProcessId, ActingProcessCommandLine
| where MaxFileSize > 2147483648   // >2GB single file written by node during extraction
| extend WriteWindowSec = datetime_diff('second', LastWrite, FirstWrite)
| order by MaxFileSize desc
#event_simpleName=FileWritten ContextImageFileName=/node(\.exe)?$/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, TargetFileName], function=sum(WrittenBytes, as=TotalBytes))
| TotalBytes > 2147483648

LightRAG destructive document ops post-bypass (DELETE /documents, clear_cache, unauth upload) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts the impact stage of CVE-2026-61740 exploitation: destructive or ingestion calls to the LightRAG document API — DELETE /documents (wipes the corpus), /documents/clear_cache (destroys LLM/embedding caches) and /documents/upload|scan|file_batch (attacker-controlled ingestion). These are inherently rare, high-consequence operations that map directly to the advisory's confidentiality/integrity/availability impact. Rationale: Uses the exact destructive LightRAG endpoints named in the advisory impact section — DELETE /documents, /documents/clear_cache, /documents/upload|scan|file_batch|texts — which are rare, high-consequence operations. The DELETE-against-/documents shape is the corpus-wipe primitive the PoC demonstrated returning HTTP 200. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-f4vv-55c2-5789 • https://cve.threatint.eu/CVE/CVE-2026-61740
ATT&CKT1485 T1496 T1190
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Administrator performing a legitimate corpus refresh (bulk /documents/upload or /documents/scan) during scheduled re-ingestion
  • Operational runbook clearing caches via /documents/clear_cache after a model/embedding change
  • A data pipeline that deletes and reloads the knowledge base on a schedule (DELETE /documents followed by re-upload)
  • CI / staging environments exercising the document API during automated tests
  • Backup/migration tooling snapshotting then clearing the document store
Suppression filter
| where RequestMethod =~ "DELETE"   // narrow to the pure corpus-destruction primitive, dropping benign upload/scan/cache-clear ingestion noise
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Identify the SourceIP and RequestMethod/RequestURL; confirm whether the call carried an X-API-Key (legitimate ops) or was preceded by a Bearer guest-JWT request (UC1) from the same IP — the latter confirms CVE-2026-61740 abuse. Check whether a change window/runbook explains it. 2) CONTAIN: Block the SourceIP, take the LightRAG instance off the public interface, and upgrade to lightrag-hku >=1.5.4; rotate TOKEN_SECRET and LIGHTRAG_API_KEY. 3) HUNT: Enumerate every document/graph/query call from that IP to quantify what was read, deleted, ingested (potential poisoning) or queried (LLM-credit exhaustion), and diff the current document store against the last backup to assess destruction.
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(24h)
| where RequestURL has "/documents"
| where (RequestMethod =~ "DELETE" and RequestURL matches regex @"(?i)/documents/?($|\?)")   // corpus wipe
     or RequestURL has_any ("/documents/clear_cache", "/documents/upload", "/documents/scan", "/documents/file_batch", "/documents/texts")   // cache destruction / attacker ingestion
| project TimeGenerated, SourceIP, DestinationIP, DestinationPort, RequestMethod, RequestURL, RequestClientApplication, DeviceAction, DeviceProduct
| order by TimeGenerated desc

LightRAG CVE-2026-61736: cross-origin credentialed read of /documents or /query (data exfil) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts credentialed cross-origin HTTP requests to LightRAG's sensitive API endpoints (/documents, /query, /query/data) where the Referer/Origin host differs from the LightRAG host — the exploitation signature of the CORS wildcard + allow_credentials flaw. A malicious site the victim visits rides their authenticated session to exfiltrate documents and knowledge-graph data. Rationale: Anchored on LightRAG's article-named endpoints (/login, /documents) plus /query corroborated by the official API docs, combined with the CORS root cause: the exploit necessarily produces a credentialed request whose Referer/Origin host != the LightRAG host. Comparing referer-host to target-host is the highest-fidelity behavioural signal the flaw leaves in access logs. Cross-checked against: • https://www.thehackerwire.com/lightrag-critical-credentialed-cors-vulnerability/ • https://github.com/HKUDS/LightRAG/blob/main/docs/LightRAG-API-Server.md • https://cve.threatint.eu/CVE/CVE-2026-61736
ATT&CKT1185 T1213
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Split frontend/backend deployment where the LightRAG SPA is served from a different host/subdomain than the API, so legitimate reads carry a cross-origin Referer
  • API gateway or reverse proxy that rewrites Host, making Referer host legitimately differ from the logged CsHost
  • Grafana/BI dashboards or internal wikis embedding LightRAG query results with their own Referer
  • Swagger UI / ReDoc API console hosted on a separate documentation host
  • DAST scanners (Burp, OWASP ZAP) issuing requests with crafted Referer values
Suppression filter
| extend RefDomain = strcat_array(array_slice(split(RefererHost,"."), -2, -1), ".") | extend TgtDomain = strcat_array(array_slice(split(TargetHost,"."), -2, -1), ".") | where RefDomain != TgtDomain
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the offending RefererHost and CIp; confirm it is NOT a sanctioned internal frontend/gateway for this LightRAG instance and check whether the CsUsername session was concurrently active from a normal client. 2) Contain: set CORS_ORIGINS to an explicit allow-list (or allow_credentials=False) and upgrade lightrag-hku to >=1.5.4; if the RefererHost is an unknown external domain, block it at the proxy/WAF and force-invalidate active LightRAG tokens. 3) Hunt: sweep all LightRAG access logs for the same RefererHost across /documents, /query, /query/data and DELETE /documents; review volume of documents/knowledge-graph rows read to scope data exposure.
AppServiceHTTPLogs
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where CsUriStem has_any ("/documents", "/query")
| where CsMethod in ("GET","POST")
| where isnotempty(Referer) and Referer != "-"
| extend RefererHost = tolower(tostring(parse_url(Referer).Host))
| extend TargetHost = tolower(tostring(split(CsHost, ":")[0]))
| where isnotempty(RefererHost) and RefererHost != TargetHost
| where RefererHost !in ("localhost", "127.0.0.1")
| project TimeGenerated, CsHost, CIp, CsUsername, CsMethod, CsUriStem, Referer, RefererHost, UserAgent, ScStatus
| order by TimeGenerated desc

LightRAG CVE-2026-61736: cross-origin DELETE of document store (destructive CORS abuse) AIActions on Objectives High
Alerts on a credentialed cross-origin DELETE against LightRAG's /documents endpoints where the Referer/Origin host differs from the LightRAG host. The advisory calls out 'deleting the entire document store' as a destructive impact of the wildcard-CORS-with-credentials flaw; a DELETE riding the victim's session from an external origin is high-fidelity data destruction. Rationale: Uses the article's explicitly stated destructive impact (delete the document store) mapped to LightRAG's real DELETE /documents and DELETE /documents/delete_document endpoints (confirmed in the API-server docs), qualified by the cross-origin Referer signature that only the CORS flaw produces — a very low-FP, high-severity combination. Cross-checked against: • https://www.thehackerwire.com/lightrag-critical-credentialed-cors-vulnerability/ • https://github.com/HKUDS/LightRAG/blob/main/docs/LightRAG-API-Server.md • https://cve.threatint.eu/CVE/CVE-2026-61736
ATT&CKT1485 T1185
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Admin performing bulk document deletion from a dedicated management console hosted on a different host than the API
  • Scheduled data-retention / cleanup jobs invoked from an orchestration host whose Referer differs from the API host
  • QA/staging teardown scripts that clear the document store between test runs
  • Split frontend deployment where the admin UI legitimately lives on another subdomain

| extend RefDomain = strcat_array(array_slice(split(RefererHost,"."), -2, -1), ".") | extend TgtDomain = strcat_array(array_slice(split(TargetHost,"."), -2, -1), ".") | where RefDomain != TgtDomain
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the RefererHost, CIp and CsUsername on the DELETE; verify whether an authorized admin intentionally cleared documents from a sanctioned console — if not, treat as active destruction. 2) Contain: immediately restrict CORS_ORIGINS to an explicit allow-list (or disable allow_credentials) and upgrade to lightrag-hku >=1.5.4; revoke/rotate the affected user's LightRAG token and, if data loss occurred, initiate restore of the document/KG store from backup. 3) Hunt: search for the same RefererHost/CIp across GET /documents and /query (pre-deletion reconnaissance/exfil) and for any /login POSTs from the same origin to establish the full session-riding chain.
AppServiceHTTPLogs
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where CsMethod == "DELETE"
| where CsUriStem has "/documents"
| where isnotempty(Referer) and Referer != "-"
| extend RefererHost = tolower(tostring(parse_url(Referer).Host))
| extend TargetHost = tolower(tostring(split(CsHost, ":")[0]))
| where isnotempty(RefererHost) and RefererHost != TargetHost
| where RefererHost !in ("localhost", "127.0.0.1")
| project TimeGenerated, CsHost, CIp, CsUsername, CsMethod, CsUriStem, Referer, RefererHost, UserAgent, ScStatus
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats summariesonly=true allow_old_summaries=true count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Web where Web.http_method="DELETE" Web.url="*/documents*" by Web.src, Web.dest, Web.dest_port, Web.url, Web.http_referrer, Web.http_user_agent, Web.status, Web.site
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| where isnotnull(http_referrer) AND http_referrer!="" AND http_referrer!="-"
| eval ref_host=lower(replace(http_referrer,"^https?:\/\/([^\/:]+).*$","\1"))
| eval site_host=lower(replace(site,":\d+$",""))
| where ref_host!=site_host AND NOT match(ref_host,"^(127\.0\.0\.1|localhost)$")
| convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| table firstTime lastTime src dest dest_port url http_referrer ref_host site_host http_user_agent status count
| sort - lastTime

Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-61736")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-61736")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc
